. We also proved that non-trivial estimation error is still possible when the latent points do not form a balanced
n
-tuple (
{\bf x}^{*}\notin\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
) to the price of an additional bias
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})
. This bias remains small compared to the
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
rate when the points have been sampled uniformly at random on
\mathcal{C}
.

We also analyzed a spectral embedding alternative in Section
4
, which benefits from a polynomial-time complexity.
When the function
f
is geometric and when the associated Fourier coefficients are suitably separated, this spectral method achieves the optimal rate
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
for uniform localization.
Yet, the spectral embedding takes advantage of the structure of Toeplitz R-matrix. Since this structure disappears in the general case of bi-Lipschitz functions, there is no apparent reason for the spectral algorithm to work over the whole class of bi-Lipschitz functions.

As our non-polynomial-time algorithm is based on an instance of the Quadratic Assignment Problem, which is known to be NP Hard and even hard to approximate, the existence of polynomial-time algorithms achieving the
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
rate over the whole class of bi-Lipschitz functions remains an open question.

The latent positions are not identifiable when
f
is unknown, and our main hypothesis is that there exists a representation
(\textbf{x},f)
with
x
close to
\Pi_{n}
and
f
bi-Lipschitz. We use as reference the regular distribution
\Pi_{n}
, since regular and uniform distributions are the ones that appear in classical models like graphon,
f
-random graphs, or statistical seriation. Our algorithms builds on this hypothesis, and consequently the bias

\textup{min}_{(\textup{{x}},f)\in\mathcal{R}(c_{l},c_{L},c_{e})}d_{\infty}(\textup{{x}},\Pi_{n})
appears in our bounds, where the minimum is over the set
\mathcal{R}(c_{l},c_{L},c_{e})
of bi-Lipschitz representatives
(\textup{{x}},f)
. This minimum leaves room to handle situations where the latent positions do not match the regular grid
\Pi_{n}
but are only more or less evenly spread. For instance, the minimal bias is zero for some representations
(\textbf{x},f)
, with
x
as far apart from
\Pi_{n}
as
d_{\infty}(\textup{{x}},\Pi_{n})\geq\pi/8
(Proposition
2.2
).
Yet, there are many practical situations where the affinity matrix is clustered, that we cannot handle. In the case where the affinity matrix is clustered (
f
bi-Lipschitz, but the
x_{i}
are clustered), the problem becomes a clustering problem, rather than a seriation problem, and our algorithms are not suited for clustering data. The question of handling simultaneously clustering and seriation is very interesting, but it is beyond the scope of this paper.

In this manuscript, we focused our attention to symmetric pairwise affinity functions
f
. However, other one-dimensional localization models such as Bradley-Terry model or more generally ranking problems, do not satisfy the symmetry assumption. Still, we hope that our general two-step approach can leverage other structural assumptions.
In ranking, a natural counterpart of our model (
3
) is the so-called SST model introduced by
[
36
]
, which is defined as follows. We observe
A_{ij}=f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})+E_{ij}
where the function
f:(x,y)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\mapsto f(x,y)\in[0,1]
is non-decreasing with respect to
x
and non-increasing with respect to
y
and satisfies the skew symmetry assumption, that is
f(x,y)=1-f(y,x)
. In this setting,
f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})
stands for the probability that player
i
wins a game against player
j
. Note that the latent space is now
[0,1]
and not the torus
\mathcal{C}
anymore. Although our methodology does not apply verbatim, we could adapt the Localize and Refine procedure for the latent space
[0,1]
. To exploit the bi-isotonic and skew-symmetric assumptions, the refinement estimator of (
16
) could for instance be replaced by

\hat{x}^{(2)}_{i}\in\argmax_{z\in G_{n_{0}}}\,\big\langle(A_{i,S}-\frac{1}{2}),z-\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}_{S}\big\rangle,\quad\textrm{for each}\ i\in\overline{S}\ ,

where
G_{n_{0}}
stands for the regular grid
\{\tfrac{1}{n_{0}},\tfrac{2}{n_{0}},\ldots,1\}
and
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}_{S}
is a suitable first-step estimator. In comparison to
(
16
),
D(z,\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}_{S})
is replaced by
z-\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}_{S}
. We expect that, with a suitable initialization
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}_{S}
and under bi-Lipschitz assumptions, the resulting procedure achieves near-optimal localization rates. This is an interesting direction for future research.

A
Some intuition on our analysis

To get some intuition on the rationale behind our analysis,
we single out the next lemma, which is a cornerstone of the analysis at least in the simplified situation where
c_{e}=0
and where the latent positions belong to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. Then, we discuss some consequences in simplified versions of our work.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\alpha,\varepsilon\geq 0
be two non-negative constants, and let
(a_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,p}
and
(d_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,p}
be two sequences fulfilling

a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\ldots\geq a_{p},\quad d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\ldots\leq d_{p},\quad\textrm{and}\quad a_{j}-a_{j+1}\geq\alpha(d_{j+1}-d_{j})-\varepsilon\,,

for
j=1,\ldots,p-1
.
Then, for any permutation
\sigma:[p]\to[p]
we have

\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}(d_{\sigma(j)}-d_{j})\geq{\alpha\over 2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}(d_{j}-d_{\sigma(j)})^{2}-\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{p}j(d_{j}-d_{\sigma(j)}).

(26)

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Let us set the notation
D_{j}=d_{1}+\ldots+d_{j}
, and

d^{\sigma}_{j}=d_{\sigma(j)}
, and
D^{\sigma}_{j}=d^{\sigma}_{1}+\ldots+d^{\sigma}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,p
.
Since
d
is non-decreasing, and since
\sigma
is a permutation of
[p]
, we have

D^{\sigma}_{j}\geq D_{j},\ \ \textrm{for}\ j=1,\ldots,p,\quad\textrm{and}\quad D^{\sigma}_{p}=D_{p}\kern 5.0pt.

Writing
d^{\sigma}_{j}=D^{\sigma}_{j}-D^{\sigma}_{j-1}
and rearranging the sums, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}(d^{\sigma}_{j}-d_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}\big((D^{\sigma}_{j}-D^{\sigma}_{j-1})-(D_{j}-D_{j-1})\big)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{a_{p}\left(D^{\sigma}_{p}-D_{p}\right)}_{=0}+\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\underbrace{(a_{j}-a_{j+1})}_{\geq\alpha(d_{j+1}-d_{j})-\varepsilon}\underbrace{(D^{\sigma}_{j}-D_{j})}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}[\alpha(d_{j+1}-d_{j})-\varepsilon(j+1-j)](D^{\sigma}_{j}-D_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}(\alpha d_{j}-\varepsilon j)(d_{j}-d^{\sigma}_{j})

\displaystyle={\alpha\over 2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}(d_{j}-d^{\sigma}_{j})^{2}-\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{p}j(d_{j}-d^{\sigma}_{j})\kern 5.0pt,

where we used Abel transformation in the penultimate line.
The proof of Lemma
A.1
is complete.
\square

Let us discuss some immediate consequences of the above lemma for our problem.
Let us consider the case where the entries
A_{ij}
of the matrix
A
decrease with
d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
for some

\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. For a fixed
i
, let
\tau
be a permutation of
[n]
such that

\big\{d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(j)}):{j=1,\ldots,n}\big\}
is ranked in increasing order. Let us set
a_{j}=A_{i\tau(j)}
and
d_{j}=d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(j)})
.
Since the entries
A_{ij}
decrease with
d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
, the sequences
(a_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,n}
and
(d_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,n}
fulfill the conditions of Lemma
A.1
with
\alpha=\varepsilon=0
.
Let us pick
k\in[n]
and let us denote by
\sigma_{k}
the permutation of
[n]
such that

d(x^{*}_{k},x^{*}_{\tau(j)})=d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(\sigma_{k}(j))})=d_{\sigma_{k}(j)}
– this is possible because
\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. We notice that
\sigma_{i}=Id
.
Then, Lemma
A.1
ensures that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{i\tau(j)}d(x^{*}_{k},x^{*}_{\tau(j)})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{i\tau(j)}d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(\sigma_{k}(j))})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{i\tau(j)}d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(j)})\kern 5.0pt,

so that

x^{*}_{i}\in\argmin_{z\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}d(z,x^{*}_{j})\kern 5.0pt.

This justifies that the criterion underlying the refined estimator (
16
) is able to recover the true latent position
x^{*}_{j}
at least in an idealized setting where the observations are noiseless, the entries of
A_{ij}
are decreasing with
d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
, and the true latent positions
x^{*}_{j}
are plugged in (
16
) instead of the initial estimator
\widehat{x}^{(1)}
.

When, in addition, we have a lower Lipschitz condition

A_{i\tau(j)}-A_{i\tau(j+1)}\geq c_{l}\left(d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(j+1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{\tau(j)})\right)\kern 5.0pt,

then, applying Lemma
A.1
, we can lower bound the difference

\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}d(x^{*}_{k},x^{*}_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\geq{c_{l}\over 2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{k},x^{*}_{j})\right)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, we observe that, for all
z\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}d(z,x^{*}_{j})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})+{c_{l}\over 2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(z,x^{*}_{j})\right)^{2},

so the sum
\sum_{j}A_{ij}d(z,x^{*}_{j})
locally increases, when
z
moves away from
x_{i}^{*}
. In the general case, where
c_{e}>0
and the observations are noisy, the criterion does not satisfy a simple local quadratic lower bound and we need to rely on finer arguments than Lemma
A.1
– see e.g. the proofs Lemma
B.3
and
B.16
.

Finally, we sketch here the proof of the second result of Proposition
2.3
, in the specific case where
c_{e}=0
. Consider any two representations
(\mathbf{x},f)
and
(\mathbf{x},f^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{R}[F,c_{l},c_{L},0]
with
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. Since both
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
belong to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
, this implies that, for any fixed
i
, the vectors
(d(x_{i},x_{j}))_{j}
and
(d(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j}))_{j}
are equal, up to a permutation of the entries. As
c_{e}=0
, the lower Lipschitz condition (
6
) ensures that
(F_{i,j})_{j=1,\ldots,n}
is decreasing both with respect to
d(x_{i},x_{j})
and
d(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})
. As a consequence, we have
d(x_{i},x_{j})=d(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})
for any
i
,
j
in
[n]
. We now show that this implies that
\mathbf{x}=Q\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
for some
Q\in\mathcal{O}
. Denote
\sigma
the permutation of
[n]
such that
\sigma(1)=1
and the arguments
\underline{x}_{j}
satisfy
\underline{x}_{\sigma(i+1)}=\underline{x}_{\sigma(i)}+2\pi/n
. As a consequence, we have
d(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\sigma(i+1)})=d(\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(i)},\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(i+1)})=2\pi/n
. This implies that either
\underline{x^{\prime}}_{\sigma(i+1)}=\underline{x^{\prime}}_{\sigma(i)}+2\pi/n
for all
i
, or
\underline{x^{\prime}}_{\sigma(i+1)}=\underline{x^{\prime}}_{\sigma(i)}-2\pi/n
for all
i
. In the former case, one easily sees that
\mathbf{x}=Q\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, where
Q
is the rotation satisfying
x_{1}=Qx^{\prime}_{1}
, whereas in the latter case, we have
\mathbf{x}=Q\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, where
Q
is the reflection satisfying
x_{1}=Qx^{\prime}_{1}
.

B
Proofs of main results

Recall that
n=4n_{0}
.
Given an orthogonal transformation
Q\in\mathcal{O}
, we define the
d_{1}
-loss relative to
Q
as

\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q):=\frac{d_{1}(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S})}{n_{0}}\kern 5.0pt.

(27)

Before proving Proposition
3.5
, we study, as a warm-up, the simpler situation where all the latent positions
x_{i}^{*}
are elements of the regular grid
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
and where the vector
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
(composed of
n_{0}
coordinates of
{\bf x}^{*}
) belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. In this case,
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})=0
.

Lemma B.1
.

In addition of the assumptions listed in Proposition
3.5
, we assume that
{\bf x}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}^{n}
and
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. Then for any
Q\in\mathcal{O}
and any
i\in\overline{S}
, the estimator (
16
) satisfies the following bound

d_{\infty}(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left(\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
.

Taking a union bound over the
n-n_{0}
indices
i\in\overline{S}
, we

d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left(\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
. This is exactly the conclusion of Proposition
3.5
in the special case where
{\bf x}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}^{n}
and
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
.
The proof of Proposition
3.5
for general
{\bf x}^{*}
follows the same scheme as that of Lemma
B.1
, but also requires some slight refinements. We first prove Lemma
B.1
before turning to the general case.

B.1
Proof of Lemma
B.1

First, we claim that it suffices to restrict our attention to transformations
Q\in
\mathcal{O}
that let
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
invariant. Indeed, for general
Q
, there exists an orthogonal transformation
Q^{\prime}
, letting
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
invariant, and
such that
\max_{z\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}}

d(Qz,Q^{\prime}z)\lesssim 1/n_{0}
. Replacing
Q^{\prime}
by
Q
in the statement of Lemma
B.1
only entails an additional term of order
1/n_{0}
which is negligible compared to the term
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
.

Let
i\in\overline{S}
. In the two next lemmas, we bound

L_{i}:=\langle F_{i,S},\,D(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},{\bf x}^{*}_{S})-D(x_{i}^{*},{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\rangle

(28)

from above and below. We recall that
F_{i,S}
is the vector (
f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*}
) for
j\in S
.

Lemma B.2
.

With probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
, we have

L_{i}\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\left(n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

for some constant
C_{L,e}>0
.

Lemma B.3
.

We have

L_{i}\geq Cnd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\Big(c_{l}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-\varepsilon_{n}\Big)-\frac{c_{e}^{3}}{\pi c_{l}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}-2\frac{c_{e}}{c_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

for some numerical constant
C>0
and all
n
larger than quantity
C_{l,e}
.

These two lemmas imply that, for
n
large enough and

d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\geq 2C^{\prime}_{l,e}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt,

with
C^{\prime}_{l,e}
large enough, we have

C^{\prime}_{l,e}nd^{2}(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq L_{i}\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\left(n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude that the error bound
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq C_{l,L,e}[\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}]
holds with probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
.

B.1.1
Proof of Lemma
B.2

Since
|S|=n_{0}
, we can assume that
S=[n_{0}]
for the ease of exposition. Let
i\in\overline{S}
. First, we decompose
L_{i}
as follows

L_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big).

The regular grid
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
is invariant by
Q
, and
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. Besides,
\hat{\bf x}_{S}^{(1)}
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. As a consequence, we have
\{x_{j}^{*};\,j\in[n_{0}]\}=\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}=

\{Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j};\,j\in[n_{0}]\}
.
Hence, we can reorder the sum in
L_{i}
as follows

L_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

To alleviate the notation, we write
\hat{z}_{i}:=Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}
and
z^{(1)}_{j}:=Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j}

so that

L_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma B.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\Big(f(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\left[n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\ .

Gathering this lemma with the definition of
L_{i}
leads us to

\displaystyle L_{i}\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})

\displaystyle\left[n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)})\right]

(29)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\left(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The orthogonal transformation
Q
preserves the distances, hence the last term of (
29
) is equal to

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big)=\langle F_{i,S},\,D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

(30)

To handle this term, we come back to the definition (
16
) of
\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}
. Since
Qx_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
, we have

\langle A_{i,S},D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq\langle A_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

This yields

\langle F_{i,S},D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq\langle E_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

The right hand-side
\langle E_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle
depends on
\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}
which belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
. This is why we simultaneously control the expression
\langle E_{i,S},

D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(z,\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle
for all
z\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
. This expression is distributed as a mean zero sub-Gaussian random variable with norm at most
C\|D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(z,\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}
. Applying a union bound over all
z\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
leads us to

\langle E_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq C\sqrt{\log(n_{0})}\|D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}

with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
. Invoking the triangular inequality for the distance
d
,
we deduce that

\|D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\leq d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\sqrt{n_{0}}
.
It follows that, with probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
,

\langle F_{i,S},\,D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq Cd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\sqrt{n_{0}\log(n_{0})}\kern 5.0pt.

Gathering this bound with
(
29
) and (
30
) concludes the proof.
\square

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

The first bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
) ensures that

|f(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})|\leq c_{L}d(z_{j}^{(1)},x^{*}_{j})+\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

By triangular inequality, we also have
|d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})|\leq d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})
so that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\Big(f(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}(c_{L}d(z_{j}^{(1)},x^{*}_{j})+\varepsilon_{n})\kern 5.0pt.

We have
d(z_{j}^{(1)},x^{*}_{j})=d(\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j},Qx^{*}_{j})
since
Q
is an orthogonal transformation. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\Big(f(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\left(c_{L}n_{0}\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+c_{e}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

∎

B.1.2
Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

An interval
I=[a,b]
denotes the set of points lying between
a
and
b
in the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
, when following the trigonometric direction from
a
to
b
. The length of
I
is denoted by
|I|
. For any point
x
in the sphere
\mathcal{C}
, its argument in
[0,2\pi)
is denoted by
\underline{x}
.

Since
|S|=n_{0}
, we can assume that
S=[n_{0}]
for the ease of exposition. Let
i\in\overline{S}
and denote
\hat{z}_{i}:=Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}.
Since
d(x_{i}^{*},\hat{z}_{i})\leq\pi
, we can assume without loss of generality that the arguments
\underline{x}_{i}^{*}=0
and
\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}\in(0,\pi]
, so that we have the equality
d(x_{i}^{*},\hat{z}_{i})=|\underline{x}_{i}^{*}-\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}|
. If
\hat{z}_{i}=x^{*}_{i}
, Lemma
B.3
is trivial. We therefore assume in the following that
\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}\in(0,\pi]
. Below, we introduce a partition of
[n_{0}]
according to the relative positions of
x_{j}^{*}
,
x_{i}^{*}
and
\hat{z}_{i}
. This partition is depicted in Figure
5
.

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\{j\in[n_{0}]:\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\in[\underline{x}^{*}_{i},\underline{\hat{z}}_{i})\}\kern 5.0pt;\quad\quad I_{2}=\{j\in[n_{0}]:\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\in[\underline{\hat{z}}_{i},\underline{x}_{i}^{*}+\pi)\}\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\{j\in[n_{0}]:\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\in[\underline{x}_{i}^{*}+\pi,\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\pi)\}\ ;\quad\quad I_{4}=\{j\in[n_{0}]:\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\in[\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\pi,\underline{x}_{i}^{*})\}\kern 5.0pt.

Fig 5
:
Partition of
[n_{0}]
in
I_{1}
–
I_{4}

Although
I_{s}
stands for a subset of indices, with a slight abuse of notation, we still write
|I_{s}|
for the length of the corresponding interval in
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
. For instance,
|I_{1}|:=|\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}|
.

We decompose
L
according to this partition of indices

L_{i}=L_{i}^{(1)}+L_{i}^{(2)}+L_{i}^{(3)}+L_{i}^{(4)}
, where
L_{i}^{(s)}
is the restriction of
L_{i}
to the set
I_{s}
. In particular, if
\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}=\pi
, then the intervals
I_{2}
and
I_{4}
are empty, and
L_{i}^{(2)}=L_{i}^{(4)}=0
.

Next, we heavily rely on the fact that the elements of
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
are evenly spaced on the sphere, that is
\{x_{i}^{*}:\,i\in[n_{0}]\}=\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
which holds true since we have assumed
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. Using the symmetry of the set
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
, we establish below that the sums
L_{i}^{(2)}
and
L_{i}^{(4)}
nearly compensate so that
L_{i}^{(2)}+L_{i}^{(4)}
admits a positive lower bound.

Lemma B.5
.

We have

L_{i}^{(2)}+L_{i}^{(4)}\geq\frac{n_{0}|I_{4}|}{2\pi}\,c_{l}d^{2}(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-n_{0}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

As for
L_{i}^{(1)}
(resp.
L_{i}^{(3)}
), we rely on the symmetry of
I_{1}
(resp.
I_{3}
) around the point of
\mathcal{C}
whose argument is
(\underline{x}^{*}_{i}+\underline{\hat{z}}_{i})/2
(resp.
((\underline{x}^{*}_{i}+\underline{\hat{z}}_{i})/2)+\pi
).

Lemma B.6
.

For some numerical constant
C>0
, we have

\displaystyle L_{i}^{(1)}+L_{i}^{(3)}\geq Cn\big(\frac{|I_{1}|}{4}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\frac{d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})}{2}(c_{l}\frac{d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})

\displaystyle-(\pi c_{l}^{2})^{-1}c_{e}^{3}\sqrt{\log^{3}(n)/n}-2\frac{c_{e}}{c_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\ .

By definition,
|I_{1}|+|I_{4}|=\pi,
which yields the desired bound

L_{i}\geq Cnd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\Big(c_{l}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-\varepsilon_{n}\Big)-\frac{c_{e}^{3}}{\pi c_{l}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}-2\frac{c_{e}}{c_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

\square

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

In Figure
5
, we can see that the difference
d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
is equal to
-d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})
for all
j\in I_{2}
, whereas it is equal to
d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})
for
j\in I_{4}.
Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle L_{i}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{2}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big)=-d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})\sum_{j\in I_{2}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle L_{i}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{4}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big)=d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})\sum_{j\in I_{4}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\phi
denote the reflection with respect to the line going through the two points of
\mathcal{C}
of arguments

a=\frac{\underline{x}_{i}^{*}+\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}}{2}\quad\textup{ and }\quad b=\frac{(\underline{x}_{i}^{*}+\pi)+(\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\pi)}{2}.

As can be checked in Figure
5
, for any
l\in I_{2}
, we have
\phi(x_{j}^{*})=x_{l}^{*}
for some
j
in
I_{4}
. Hence,

L_{i}^{(2)}+L_{i}^{(4)}=d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})\sum_{j\in I_{4}}\big(f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))\big)\kern 5.0pt.

To lower bound the difference in the sum , we invoke the bi-Lipschitz condition (
6
), which gives

f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))\geq c_{l}(d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j}))-\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt,

since
x_{j}^{*}
is closer to
x_{i}^{*}
than
\phi(x_{j}^{*})
– see again Figure
5
.
Also, we can check from Figure
5
that
d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})=d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})
for all
j\in I_{4}
. Since
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
is evenly spaced, the number of indices
j
in
I_{4}
is larger than
n_{0}|I_{4}|/(2\pi)
. This leads us to

L_{i}^{(2)}+L_{i}^{(4)}\geq\frac{n_{0}|I_{4}|}{2\pi}\,d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})c_{l}d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})-n_{0}d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})=d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})
, this concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Lemma
B.6
.

From Figure
5
, we see that, for all
j\in I_{1}
,

d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})=|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|-|\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|=\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\kern 5.0pt.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, write
I_{1}^{(\alpha)}
the sub-interval of
I_{1}
defined as

I_{1}^{(\alpha)}=\left\{j\in[n_{0}]:\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\in\left[\underline{x}_{i}^{*},(1-\alpha)\underline{x}_{i}^{*}+\alpha\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}\right)\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, for all
j\in I^{(1/2)}_{1}
, the above expression leads us to

d(\hat{z}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))-d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))=-\big(\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x^{*}_{j}}\big)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\phi
is the symmetry introduced in the proof of Lemma
B.5
. Hence, the terms with
j\in I^{(1/2)}_{1}
partially compensate with the terms with
j
outside
I^{(1/2)}_{1}
.

\displaystyle L_{i}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I_{1}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\big[d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)}}\big[f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))\big]\big[\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x}^{*}_{j}\big]\kern 5.0pt.

For any
j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)}
, we have
d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))\geq d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
. As a consequence, it follows from the bi-Lipschitz condition (
6
) that

f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))\geq c_{l}\left[d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\right]-\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
d(x^{*}_{i},\phi(x^{*}_{j}))-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})=|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|
for all
j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)}
, we get

L_{i}^{(1)}\geq\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)}}(c_{l}|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x}^{*}_{j})\kern 5.0pt.

(31)

To control (
31
), we split the interval
I_{1}^{(1/2)}
according to the sign of the term
(c_{l}|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})
. That is, we write
I_{1}^{(1/2)}=I_{1}^{(1/2)-}\cup I_{1}^{(1/2)+}
where
I_{1}^{(1/2)-}
is the set of indices
j
such that
c_{l}|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|<\varepsilon_{n}
.

Claim B.7
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)-}}(c_{l}|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})

\displaystyle(\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x}^{*}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq-(c_{l}^{2}8\pi)^{-1}c_{e}^{3}\sqrt{\log^{3}(n)/n}-\frac{c_{e}}{c_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

Claim B.8
.

For some numerical constant
C>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)+}}(c_{l}|\underline{\phi(x^{*}_{j})}-\underline{x}^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})

\displaystyle(\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}+\underline{x}^{*}_{i}-2\underline{x}^{*}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq Cn\big(\frac{|I_{1}|}{4}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\big)\frac{|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{i}|}{2}(c_{l}\frac{|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{i}|}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})\kern 5.0pt.

Gathering these two claims leads us to

\displaystyle L_{i}^{(1)}\geq Cn\big(\frac{|I_{1}|}{4}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\frac{|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{i}|}{2}(c_{l}\frac{|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{i}|}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})

\displaystyle-(c_{l}^{2}8\pi)^{-1}c_{e}^{3}\sqrt{\log^{3}(n)/n}-\frac{c_{e}}{c_{l}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\ ,

which is the desired bound since
|\underline{\hat{z}}_{i}-\underline{x}^{*}_{i}|=d(\hat{z}_{i},x^{*}_{i})=d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})
. By symmetry, the term
L_{i}^{(3)}
is handled as
L_{i}^{(1)}
and admits the same lower bound.
∎

Proof of Claim
B.7
.

For simplicity, the notation
\underline{x}
is dropped out in the proof of Claim
B.7
, and
\underline{x}
is simply denoted by
x
. By definition of
\phi
, we know that
(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i})/2=(\phi(x^{*}_{j})+x^{*}_{j})/2
for all
j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)},
which gives the equality
\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j}=\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}
. Since
0\leq\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}<c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}
for all
j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)-}
, we have

0\leq\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j}\leq c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n}<0
for
j
in
I_{1}^{(1/2)-}
, we obtain

(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j})\geq(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\geq-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the number of indices in
I_{1}^{(1/2)-}
is at most
1+n_{0}/(2\pi)|I_{1}^{(1/2)-}|
(where
|I_{1}^{(1/2)-}|
is the arc length), and the length of this arc is at most
c_{l}^{-1}\,\varepsilon_{n}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)-}}(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{n_{0}}{2\pi}c_{l}^{-2}\varepsilon_{n}^{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{e}^{3}}{8\pi c_{l}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}-c_{l}^{-1}\epsilon_{n}\ .

∎

Proof of Claim
B.8
.

Again, for convenience the notation
\underline{x}
is dropped out here. Since all the terms in the sum are nonnegative, we can simply consider indices
j
in
I_{1}^{(1/2)+}\cap I_{1}^{(1/4)}
. Using
\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}=\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j}
for all
j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)}
and
x_{i}^{*}=0
, we obtain that, for
j\in I_{1}^{(1/4)}
,
\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}\geq\hat{z}_{i}/2
. This gives

(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j})\geq(c_{l}\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}\kern 5.0pt,

and, for some numerical constant
C>0
,

\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)+}}(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j})\geq Cn\big|I_{1}^{(1/2)+}\cap I_{1}^{(1/4)}\big|\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}(c_{l}\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})\kern 5.0pt.

(32)

Since either
I_{1}^{(1/2)+}\subset I_{1}^{(1/4)}
or
I_{1}^{(1/4)}\subset I_{1}^{(1/2)+}
and
|I_{1}^{(1/4)}|=|I_{1}|/4
and
|I_{1}^{(1/2)+}|=|I_{1}^{(1/2)}|-|I_{1}^{(1/2)-}|\geq|I_{1}^{(1/2)}|-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}=\frac{|I_{1}|}{2}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}
, we deduce that

|I_{1}^{(1/2)+}\cap I_{1}^{(1/4)}|\geq\frac{|I_{1}|}{4}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we have

\sum_{j\in I_{1}^{(1/2)+}}(c_{l}|\phi(x^{*}_{j})-x^{*}_{j}|-\varepsilon_{n})(\hat{z}_{i}+x^{*}_{i}-2x^{*}_{j})\geq Cn\big(\frac{|I_{1}|}{4}-c_{l}^{-1}\varepsilon_{n}\big)\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}(c_{l}\frac{\hat{z}_{i}}{2}-\varepsilon_{n})\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\hat{z}_{i}=|\hat{z}_{i}-x_{i}^{*}|
(recall that
x_{i}^{*}=0
), this concludes the proof.
∎

B.2
Proof of Proposition
3.5

Let
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
be a best approximation of
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}},
that is, such that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},{\bf x}^{**}_{S})=d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
. As in the proof of Lemma
B.1
, we restrict our attention to orthogonal transformations
Q\in\mathcal{O}
that let
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
invariant.
Fix
i
in
\overline{S}
. To prove Proposition
3.5
, it suffices to establish variants of Lemmas
B.2
and

B.3
with

\widetilde{L}_{i}:=\langle F_{i,S},\,D(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},{\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(x_{i}^{*},{\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle,

(33)

instead of
L_{i}
. In the definition of
\widetilde{L}_{i}
,
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
has been replaced by
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
.

Lemma B.9
.

With probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
, we have

\widetilde{L}_{i}\leq C_{L,e}\left[1+d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\big(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\,\big)\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma B.10
.

For
n
large enough, one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{l,e}nd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\Big(d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n}\Big)-C^{\prime\prime}_{l,e}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}

\displaystyle-C_{L,e}\left\{1+n\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

These two lemmas enforce that, with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
,

d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, assume that
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\geq C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}\big(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\big)
where
C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}
is large enough. Then, Lemma
B.10
implies that
\widetilde{L}_{i}\gtrsim_{c_{l},c_{L},c_{e}}nd^{2}(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})
. Together with Lemma
B.9
, we deduce that

d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\right]\ .

In any case, we conclude that

d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\leq(C_{l,L,e}\vee C^{\prime}_{l,L,e})\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\right]\ ,

with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
. Taking the minimum over all
Q\in\mathcal{O}
that let
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
invariant and a union bound over all
i\in\overline{S}
, leads to Proposition
3.5
.

B.2.1
Proof of Lemma
B.9

To ease the exposition, we assume that
S=[n_{0}]
. Fix
i\in\overline{S}
. We start from

\widetilde{L}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

(34)

In order to come back to the setting of Lemma
B.1
, we replace
f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})
by
f(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})
, using the bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
) so that

f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\leq(c_{L}\vee c_{e})\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

By triangular inequality, we have
d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\leq d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{*}_{i})
which implies

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\Big(f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(c_{L}\vee c_{e})d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})r_{n}\kern 5.0pt,

where we define
r_{n}=nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}
. This leads us to

\widetilde{L}_{i}\leq(c_{L}\vee c_{e})d(\hat{x}^{(2)}_{i},Qx^{*}_{i})r_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
x^{**}_{j}
now runs over
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, we can replace as in the proof of Lemma
B.2
the sum over
x_{j}^{**}
by a sum over
Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j}
using a suitable permutation:

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{i}\leq(c_{L}\vee c_{e})

\displaystyle d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})r_{n}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})\Big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})-d(x^{*}_{i},Q^{-1}\hat{x}^{(1)}_{j})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

The remainder of the proof follows the same lines as for Lemma
B.2
, except for small differences. Still, we provide some details for the sake of completeness. as in that proof we write
\hat{z}_{i}=Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}
and
z^{(1)}_{i}=Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)}
. We first apply Lemma
B.4
to obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{i}\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}^{(2)}_{i},Qx^{*}_{i})

\displaystyle\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x_{j}^{*})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z^{(1)}_{j})\Big)\kern 5.0pt.

The last expression simplifies in

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x_{j}^{*})\Big(d(\hat{z}_{i},z_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{*}_{i},z^{(1)}_{j})\Big)=\langle F_{i,S},\,D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

In Lemma
B.2
, we had
Qx^{*}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
so that we could use the definition of
\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}
to
deduce that
\langle A_{i,S},D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq\langle A_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle
.
Unfortunately,
Qx^{*}_{i}
does not necessarily belong to
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
anymore. To handle this minor issue, we replace
x^{*}_{i}
by the closest element
y^{*}_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
. It satisfies
d(x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i})\leq 2\pi/n_{0}
and
Qy^{*}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
. This leads us to

\displaystyle\langle A_{i,S},D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle A_{i,S},D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle A_{i,S},D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle+\langle A_{i,S},D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

Since
|d(Qy_{i}^{*},x_{i})-d(Qx_{i}^{*},x_{i})|\leq 2\pi/n_{0}
and
|f(x,y)|\leq 1
, the above additional error term satisfies

\displaystyle\langle A_{i,S},D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle F_{i,S},D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle E_{i,S},D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi+

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(n)}\|D(Qy_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\kern 5.0pt,

with probability higher than
1-1/(2n^{3})
. Putting everything together, we have shown that

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{i}\leq C_{L,e}d(\hat{x}^{(2)}_{i},Qx^{*}_{i})

\displaystyle\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+n\mu_{{\hat{\bf x}}^{(1)}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}}(Q)+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]

\displaystyle+C+\langle E_{i,S},\,D(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D(Qx_{i}^{*},\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\kern 5.0pt,

with probability higher than
1-1/(2n^{3})
. To conclude, it suffices to the rhs in the above expression. We do it exactly as in the end of the proof of Lemma
B.2
except that we now consider a probability
1-1/(2n^{3})
.

B.2.2
Proof of Lemma
B.10
.

Fix
i\in\overline{S}
and define
y^{*}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
as a closest point to
x_{i}^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
.
We introduce the quantity

L^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(y^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(y^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big)\kern 5.0pt,

which has the same properties as the
L_{i}
used in Lemma
B.1
, since each point involved in the expression of
L_{i}^{\prime}
is an element of
\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}
, and the sum runs over a vector
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. This allows us to invoke Lemma
B.3
–from the proof of Lemma
B.1
– to get

L^{\prime}_{i}\geq Cnd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qy^{*}_{i})\left[c_{l}d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qy^{*}_{i})-\varepsilon_{n}\right]-\frac{c_{e}^{3}}{\pi c_{l}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

for
n
large enough.

By definition of
y_{i}^{*}
, we know that

d(y^{*}_{i},x_{i}^{*})\leq 2\pi/n_{0}
. Hence, by triangular inequality,
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qy^{*}_{i})\geq d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-2\pi/n_{0}
and we derive that

L_{i}^{\prime}\geq C^{\prime}_{l,e}nd(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\left[d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})-\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]-C^{\prime\prime}_{l,e}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

Next, we rely on the following lemma to replace
L_{i}^{\prime}
by
\widetilde{L}_{i}
.

Lemma B.11
.

We have

|\widetilde{L}_{i}-L_{i}^{\prime}|\leq C_{L,e}\left[1+\big(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\big)d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},Qx^{*}_{i})\right]\ .

Gathering these two bounds completes the proof of Lemma
B.10
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
B.11
.

From the definition of
y_{i}^{*}
and the triangular inequality, we have

\Big|d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})-d(y^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big|\leq 2\pi/n_{0}\kern 5.0pt.

This allows us to deduce that the quantity

L^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\big(d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},x^{**}_{j})-d(y^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big)

(35)

satisfies
|\widetilde{L}_{i}-L^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq 2\pi
.
Besides, we deduce from the bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
) and the triangular inequality that

\displaystyle|f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(y^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{L}\left(d(x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i})+d(x^{*}_{j},x^{**}_{j})\right)+2\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{L}d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+2\varepsilon_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we deduce that

|L^{\prime}_{i}-L_{i}^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2nd(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},y^{*}_{i})\big(c_{L}d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\varepsilon_{n}\big)\kern 5.0pt.

All in all, we have

\displaystyle|\widetilde{L}_{i}-L_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq|\widetilde{L}_{i}-L_{i}^{\prime\prime}|+|L_{i}^{\prime\prime}-L_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{L,e}\left[1+d(Q^{-1}\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)},y^{*}_{i})\big(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\big)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

and the result follows.
∎

B.3
Proof of Proposition
3.6

B.3.1
Main Arguments

Assume that
S=[n_{0}]
for the ease of presentation. In this proof, we both interpret
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
and
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
as vectors in
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and matrices of size
2\times n_{0}
. We recall that
\|.\|_{q}
refers to the entry-wise
l_{q}
norm for matrices.
We shall establish that the estimator
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
is such that the matrix
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)T}_{S}\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
is close to
{\bf x}^{*T}_{S}{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
. In other words, the distances between
\hat{x}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\hat{x}_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
are close to the respective distances between the
x^{*}_{1},\ldots,x^{*}_{n_{0}}
. Then, relying on a recent matrix perturbation result from
[
1
]
, we deduce that, up to an orthogonal transformation,
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
and
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
are close. Let us
first state this perturbation result. Given any
p\times 2
matrix
M
with real coefficients, we denote its transpose by
M^{T}
, and the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse by
M^{\dagger}
, and the usual operator norm by
\|M\|_{op}
. In this proof, the transformations
Q\in\mathcal{O}
are interpreted as orthogonal matrices of size
2\times 2
.

Proposition B.12

(Theorem 1 in
[
1
]
)
.

For any positive integer
p
and any
p\times 2
matrices
M
and
N
, with
N
having full rank, let
\nu=\|MM^{T}-NN^{T}\|_{2}
. Then, we have

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\,\|M-NQ\|_{2}\lesssim\nu\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}\kern 5.0pt,

as soon as
2\nu\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}\leq 1
.

Let
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
denote a best approximation of
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}},
so that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},{\bf x}^{**}_{S})=d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
. In order to invoke the above proposition for
M=(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})^{T}
and
N=({\bf x}^{**}_{S})^{T}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times 2}
, we need to check that the condition
2\nu\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}\leq 1
is fulfilled. First, to bound the term
\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}
, we work out

\displaystyle N^{\dagger}=(N^{T}N)^{-1}N^{T}=\left({\bf x}^{**}_{S}{\bf x}_{S}^{**T}\right)^{-1}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}({\bf x}^{**}_{1i})^{2}&0\\
0&\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}({\bf x}^{**}_{2i})^{2}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n_{0}}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\kern 5.0pt,

since
\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}({\bf x}^{**}_{1i})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}({\bf x}^{**}_{2i})^{2}=n_{0}/2
– see e.g. (
76
) for a proof.
As a consequence,

\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}\leq\|N^{\dagger}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{4}{n_{0}}\ .

The following lemma bounds
\nu=\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)T}_{S}\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**T}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{2}
.

Lemma B.13
.

With probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
, we have

\nu\leq C_{l,L,e}\left(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
, we obtain

2\nu\|N^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}\leq C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}\left(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

If
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\geq 1/C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}
, the conclusion of Proposition
3.6
obviously holds since
\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{1}(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\leq n_{0}\leq n
. Hence, it suffices to consider the case where
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\leq 1/C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}
so that the condition of Proposition
B.12
is fulfilled. This implies

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\,\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)T}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**T}Q\|_{2}\lesssim C\left(\sqrt{n}d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

and so

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\,\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{2}C\left(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Since the distances in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathcal{C}
are equivalent, we have
\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim d_{1}(Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S})=d_{1}({\bf x}^{**}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S})
. Then, using the definition of
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
, we get
\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
. Together with the triangular inequality, this leads us to

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\,\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Using again the equivalence between the distances, that is
d(x,y)\lesssim\|x-y\|_{1}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, we conclude that

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}d_{1}(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S},Q{\bf x}_{S}^{*})\leq C\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\,\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}\|_{1}\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

and the proof of Proposition
3.6
is complete.

B.3.2
Proof of Lemma
B.13

Both
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
and
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
are elements of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
, hence they both satisfy
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}1=0
and
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}1=0
where
1
denotes the vector of ones. Indeed, since
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}e^{\iota 2\pi k/n_{0}}=0
, we have
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\cos(2\pi k/n_{0})=0
and
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\sin(2\pi k/n_{0})=0
. We can then invoke the next lemma to bound
\nu=\|\hat{\bf x}^{(1)T}_{S}\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**T}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{2}.

Lemma B.14
.

For any
Z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n_{0}})
and
Z^{\prime}=(z^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{n_{0}})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
with
Z1=Z^{\prime}1=0
, let
D=(D_{ij})
and
D^{\prime}=(D_{ij}^{\prime})
be their (squared) distance matrices, that is
D_{ij}=\|z_{i}-z_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
and
D_{ij}^{\prime}=\|z^{\prime}_{i}-z^{\prime}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
for all
i,j\in[n_{0}].
Then we have

\|Z^{T}Z-Z^{\prime T}Z^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|D-D^{\prime}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

For
Z={\bf x}^{**}_{S}
and
Z^{\prime}=\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
, and accordingly
D_{ij}=\|x^{**}_{i}-x^{**}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
and
D_{ij}^{\prime}=\|\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)}-\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}
, it follows from Lemma
B.14
that

\nu\leq\|D-D^{\prime}\|_{2}
. Since all square distances
D_{ij}
and
D^{\prime}_{ij}
are at most equal to
4
, we get

\nu\leq 4\|\sqrt{D}-\sqrt{D^{\prime}}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\sqrt{D}
and
\sqrt{D^{\prime}}
denote the matrices of coefficients
\sqrt{D_{ij}}=\|x^{**}_{i}-x^{**}_{j}\|_{2}
and
\sqrt{D_{ij}^{\prime}}=\|\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)}-\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)}\|_{2}
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, elementary geometry gives
\|x-y\|_{2}=2\sin(d(x,y)/2)
. Since the sinus function is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\big|\,\|x-y\|_{2}-\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|_{2}\big|\leq|d(x,y)-d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\kern 5.0pt,

for any
x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. Hence, we deduce that
\nu\leq 4\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}
,
where
D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})
and
D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})
respectively denote the matrices of coefficients
d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})
and
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})
. As a consequence, we mainly have to control with high probability
\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}
.

Lemma B.15
.

With probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
, we have

\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Hence
\nu\leq C_{l,L,e}[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}]
and the proof of Lemma
B.13
is complete.
\square

Proof of Lemma
B.14
.

Let
H=I-J/n_{0}
, where
I
is the identity and
J
the matrix of ones. Since
Z1=0
, we have
ZH=Z
, so that

Z^{T}Z=HZ^{T}ZH=-\frac{1}{2}HDH,

since
D
is the matrix of distances associated with
Z
. Then we have

\|Z^{T}Z-Z^{\prime T}Z^{\prime}\|_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|H(D-D^{\prime})H\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|D-D^{\prime}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality derives from the general relation
\|AB\|_{2}\leq\|A\|_{op}\|B\|_{2}
for any matrices
A,B,
and the fact that
\|H\|_{op}=1
– because
H
is an orthogonal projection. Lemma
B.14
is proved.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.15
.

First, we come back to the definition of the estimator
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
defined in (
18
).
We have
\langle A_{S,S},D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle\leq\langle A_{S,S},D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle
, which implies that

\langle F_{S,S},D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle\leq\langle E_{S,S},D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle.

As in the last lines of the proof of Lemma
B.2
, we bound the term
\langle E_{S,S},D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle
by a union bound over all possible vectors
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\langle F_{S,S},D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle E_{S,S},D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(n)}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

(36)

with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
.
Conversely, we shall lower bound
\langle F_{S,S},D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle
.

\langle F_{S,S},D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)\ .

Using the bi-Lipschitz property of the function
f
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\big|f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{L}\left(d(x^{*}_{i},x^{**}_{i})+d(x^{*}_{j},x^{**}_{j})\right)+2c_{e}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{L}d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+2c_{e}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt,

by definition of
{\bf x}^{**}
. Then, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}\big|f(x^{*}_{i},x^{*}_{j})-f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big|\big|d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{L,e}\left(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\,\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L,e}n_{0}\left(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt\ ,

where we applied Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on
\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times n_{0}}
.
As a consequence,

\displaystyle\langle F_{S,S},

\displaystyle D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})-D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})\rangle\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle-C_{L,e}n\left(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\,\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

(37)

The following result bounds
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})[d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})]
in terms of the Frobenius norm
\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}
. This is a key step in our proof. Had the slack constant
c_{e}
been equal to zero, the following result would have been a consequence of Lemma
A.1
. Here the proof is slightly more involved and is provided below.

Lemma B.16
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\Big(d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})

\displaystyle\Big)\geq\frac{c_{l}}{2}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-c_{e}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}.

We conclude from (
B.3.2
) and the above lemma that

\displaystyle C\sqrt{n\log(n)}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})

\displaystyle-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\geq\frac{c_{l}}{2}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle-C_{L,e}\left(nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}

which in turn implies that

\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime}_{l,L,e}\left[nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma
B.15
is proved.

∎

Proof of Lemma
B.16
.

To alleviate the notation, we introduce

\gamma_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}f(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})[d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})]

so that we aim at establishing a lower bound for each
\gamma_{i}
and in turn for
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\gamma_{i}
. To simplify the arguments, we only consider the case where
n_{0}
is odd, the case of
n_{0}
even being almost similar.

Both
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
and
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
and we shall heavily rely on the symmetries of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. Assume without loss of generality that
i=1
and
x^{**}_{j}=e^{\iota 2\pi(j-1)/n_{0}}
for all
j=1,\ldots,n_{0}
. Then,
d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})=\tfrac{2\pi}{n_{0}}[|j-1|\wedge|n_{0}-j+1|]
. Since
\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S}
also belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, there exists a permutation
\sigma
of
[n_{0}]
such that
\sigma(1)=1
and
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})=\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}[|\sigma(j)-1|\wedge|n_{0}+1-\sigma(j)|]
.
Recall that we consider the case where
n_{0}
is odd. Besides, we can focus on
n_{0}
larger than
3
since Lemma
B.16
is trivial for
n_{0}=1
. Thus, there exists a surjective map
\overline{\sigma}:[n_{0}-1]\mapsto[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]
such that
|\overline{\sigma}^{-1}(\{z\})|=2
for any
z\in[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]
and
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})=\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}\overline{\sigma}(j-1)
for any
j=2,\ldots n_{0}
.
Finally, we write
\psi_{j}=f(1,e^{\iota 2\pi j/n_{0}})
and
\psi^{\prime}_{j}=f(1,e^{-\iota 2\pi j/n_{0}})
for
j=1,\ldots\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor
.
Equipped with this new notation, we arrive at

\gamma_{i}=\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}\psi_{j}[\overline{\sigma}(j)-j]+\psi^{\prime}_{j}[\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j]\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, we denote

a_{j}=\overline{\sigma}(j)-j
and
a^{\prime}_{j}=\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j
for
j=1,\ldots,\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor
. Obviously, we have
\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}a_{j}+a^{\prime}_{j}=0
. More generally, one easily checks that, for any positive integer
s\leq\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor
,
the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{s}(a_{j}+a^{\prime}_{j})
is nonnegative. Starting from

\gamma_{i}=\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}\psi_{j}a_{j}+\psi^{\prime}_{j}a^{\prime}_{j}

we partition the indices according to the signs of
a_{j}
and
a^{\prime}_{j}
.
Define
A_{+}=\{j\in[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]:a_{j}\geq 0\}
,
A_{-}=\{j\in[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]:a_{j}<0\}
,
A^{\prime}_{+}=\{j\in[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]:a^{\prime}_{j}\geq 0\}
, and
A^{\prime}_{-}=\{j\in[\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor]:a^{\prime}_{j}<0\}
. Intuitively, we want to group indices
j
such that
a_{j}>0
with indices
k
such that
a_{k}<0
. This can be done by recursion. First, consider the smallest index
k\in A_{-}\cup A^{\prime}_{-}
. By symmetry, suppose that
a_{k}<0
. Since
\sum_{j=1}^{k}(a_{j}+a^{\prime}_{j})\geq 0
, this implies that
\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{j\in A_{+}}a_{j}+\mathbf{1}_{j\in A^{\prime}_{+}}a_{j}^{\prime}\geq|a_{k}|+\mathbf{1}_{k\in A^{\prime}_{-}}|a_{k}^{\prime}|
. Hence, it is possible to build nonnegative numbers
b_{j,k,1}\leq a_{j}
for
j\in A_{+}\cap[k]
and
b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}\leq a^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in A^{\prime}_{+}\cap[k]
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{j\in A_{+}}b_{j,k,1}+\mathbf{1}_{j\in A^{\prime}_{+}}b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}=|a_{k}|
. Iterating the construction we obtain the following decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{n_{0}}{2\pi}\gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A_{+}}\left(\sum_{k\in A_{-}}(\psi_{j}-\psi_{k})b_{j,k,1}+\sum_{k\in A^{\prime}_{-}}(\psi_{j}-\psi^{\prime}_{k})b_{j,k,2}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}_{+}}\left(\sum_{k\in A_{-}}(\psi^{\prime}_{j}-\psi_{k})b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}+\sum_{k\in A^{\prime}_{-}}(\psi^{\prime}_{j}-\psi^{\prime}_{k})b^{\prime}_{j,k,2}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where all
b_{j,k,t}
’s are nonnegative,
b_{j,k,t}=0
for
k<j
, and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{k\in A_{-}}b_{j,k,1}+\sum_{k\in A^{\prime}_{-}}b_{j,k,2}=a_{j}\text{ for }j\in A_{+}\ ;\\
\sum_{k\in A_{-}}b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}+\sum_{k\in A^{\prime}_{-}}b^{\prime}_{j,k,2}=a^{\prime}_{j}\text{ for }j\in A^{\prime}_{+}\ ;\\
\sum_{j\in A_{+}}b_{j,k,1}+\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}_{+}}b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}=-a_{k}\text{ for }k\in A_{-}\ ;\\
\sum_{j\in A_{+}}b_{j,k,2}+\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}_{+}}b^{\prime}_{j,k,2}=-a^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for }k\in A^{\prime}_{-}\ .\end{array}\right.

In the above decomposition all the terms
b_{j,k,1}
,
b_{j,k,2}
,
b^{\prime}_{j,k,1}
, and
b^{\prime}_{j,k,2}
are nonnegative. Besides, they are positive only when
k\geq j
, so that we can use the bi-Lipschitz condition (
6
)

(\psi_{j}-\psi^{\prime}_{k})=f(1,e^{\iota 2\pi(j-1)/n_{0}})-f(1,e^{-\iota 2\pi(k-1)/n_{0}})\geq c_{l}\frac{2\pi(k-j)}{n_{0}}-c_{e}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\ .

We obtain similarly the same lower bound for
\psi_{j}-\psi_{k}
,
\psi^{\prime}_{j}-\psi_{k}
, and
\psi^{\prime}_{j}-\psi^{\prime}_{k}
. Coming back to the expression
\gamma_{j}
and the definition of the
b_{i,j,t}
with
t=1,2
yields

\displaystyle\frac{n_{0}}{2\pi}\gamma_{i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{l}\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}-j\left[a_{j}+a^{\prime}_{j}\right]-c_{e}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}|a_{j}|+|a^{\prime}_{j}|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-c_{l}\frac{2\pi}{n_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}\left[j(\overline{\sigma}(j)-j)+j(\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j)\right]

\displaystyle-c_{e}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}|\overline{\sigma}(j)-j|+|\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j|\ .

Let us work out these two expressions in the rhs. By symmetry and definition of
\overline{\sigma}
and
\sigma
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}-

\displaystyle j(\overline{\sigma}(j)-j)-j(\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}(\overline{\sigma}(j)-j)^{2}+(\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{0}^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\left[d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we get

\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}|\sigma(j)-j|+|\overline{\sigma}(n_{0}-j)-j|=\frac{n_{0}}{2\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}|d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})|\kern 5.0pt.

Putting everything together yields

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{l}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\left[d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle-c_{e}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}|d(\hat{x}_{i}^{(1)},\hat{x}_{j}^{(1)})-d(x^{**}_{i},x^{**}_{j})|\kern 5.0pt,

which in turn allows us to conclude

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{l}}{2}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}-c_{e}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{l}}{2}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}-c_{e}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\|D({\bf x}^{**}_{S})-D(\hat{\bf x}^{(1)}_{S})\|_{2}\ .

Lemma
B.16
is proved.
∎

B.4
Proof of Theorem
3.1
and
3.2

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.2
. Theorem
3.1
then follows directly from this result.

B.4.1
Main arguments

Recall that
n=4n_{0}
. For
n_{0}\leq 3
, the bound of Theorem
3.2
is trivially true. Assume that
n_{0}\geq 4
in the following. In Step 1 of the main procedure, it follows from Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
that the output
\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}}^{(2)}
satisfies the following uniform bound

\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

(38)

with probability higher than
1-2/n^{2}
. Similarly, for the output
\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}}
in Step 2, we have

\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

(39)

with probability higher than
1-2/n^{2}
. In (
38
) and (
39
), we shall prove that the bias terms
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
and
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
are of the same order as
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})
up to an additional error of the order of
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
– see Lemma
B.17
below.

Lemma B.17
.

Assume that
n_{0}\geq 4
and fix
{\bf x}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}
. There exists an event of probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
such that

\displaystyle d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq C\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\ ;

\displaystyle d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq C\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2n_{0}})\leq C\left[d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, by a union bound, the following inequalities hold together with probability at least
1-5/n^{2}
:

\exists\,Q_{1}\in\mathcal{O}:\qquad d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\,\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

(40)

\exists\,Q_{2}\in\mathcal{O}:\qquad d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(41)

Since the final estimator
{\bf\hat{x}}:=({\bf\hat{x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}},\widehat{Q}{\bf\hat{x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}})
satisfies
\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}}=\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}}
, we deduce from (
40
)
that

d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

To prove Theorem
3.2
, it suffices to show the counterpart of this bound on
S
:

d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{S},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(42)

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{S},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{S},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})+d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{S},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})

\displaystyle=d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{S},Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})+\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)\kern 5.0pt,

(43)

since
\hat{{\bf x}}_{S}=\widehat{Q}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{S}
by definition of
\hat{{\bf x}}
. By (
41
) and since
S\subset\overline{S}^{\prime}
, we have

d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{S},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

In view of (
42
) and (
B.4.1
), it remains to prove that

\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)\leq C_{l,L,e}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\ .

(44)

Before consider this maximum, we control the quantity
d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})
that will turn out to be instrumental. By the triangular inequality,

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})+d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(\widehat{Q}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})\kern 5.0pt.

By definition of
\widehat{Q}
, the second term of the right hand-side is bounded by
d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}Q^{-1}_{2}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})
, which, in turn, is bounded as follows

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}Q^{-1}_{2}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})+d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}Q^{-1}_{2}\hat{{\bf x}}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})\kern 5.0pt.

Together with (
40
) and (
41
), this leads us to

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2d_{\infty}(\hat{\bf x}^{(2)}_{\overline{S}},Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})+2d_{\infty}(\hat{\bf x}^{(2^{\prime})}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}^{\prime}})

(45)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{lLe}\left[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now come back to proving (
44
). Since the symmetric group on the plane is only made of rotations and reflections, we consider two cases.

Case 1:
Q_{1}^{-1}\widehat{Q}Q_{2}
is a rotation.
Then,
d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)
does not depend on
y
. In particular,
\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)=d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})
and (
44
) is a consequence of (
45
).

Case 2:
Q_{1}^{-1}\widehat{Q}Q_{2}
is a reflection
. Then,
\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)=\pi
.

If
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}^{-1}\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})\geq\pi/4
, then

\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(Q_{1}y,\widehat{Q}Q_{2}y)\leq 4d_{\infty}(Q_{1}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})\kern 5.0pt,

and (
44
) is again a consequence of (
45
). If
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},Q_{1}^{-1}\widehat{Q}Q_{2}{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}})\leq\pi/4
, this implies that the points in
{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}}
belong to two arcs of length
\pi/4
that are (individually) symmetric around the axis of the reflection
Q_{1}^{-1}\widehat{Q}Q_{2}
. It follows that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2n_{0}})\geq\pi/8
as soon as
2n_{0}\geq 4
, that is
n\geq 8
. Indeed, if
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2n_{0}})<\pi/8
and
2n_{0}\geq 4
, this would imply that, any point on
\mathcal{C}
is at distance less than
3\pi/8
from
{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}}
which is impossible because those points in
{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}}
belong to these two arcs of length
\pi/4
.
Since Lemma
B.17
ensures that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2n_{0}})
is of the same order as
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})
, this implies that the latter is of the order of a constant and (
44
) is obviously valid.

B.4.2
Proof of Lemma
B.17

We claim that it suffices to restrict our attention to the case where
{\bf x}^{*}=(x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*})
are
n
distinct points. Indeed, for general points
x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exist points
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
that are all distinct and satisfy
d(y_{j},x_{j}^{*})\leq 1/n
for all
j\in[n]
. Replacing
x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*}
by
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
in the statement of Lemma
B.17
only entails an additional term
1/n
which is negligible compared to the term
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
.

For any
k\in[n]
and any vector
\textbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
, we introduce a new quantity that is equivalent to
d_{\infty}(\textbf{x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k})
, but more easy to handle. For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
, we write
N_{I}(\textbf{x})
the number of coordinates of
x
that lie in the interval
I
, i.e. the number of
i\in[k]
such that
\underline{x}_{i}\in I
. We then define the quantity
V_{I}(\textbf{x})
as

V_{I}(\textbf{x})=N_{I}(\textbf{x})-k\frac{|I|}{2\pi}\kern 5.0pt.

(46)

Remark that, for a uniform
k
-sample of
\mathcal{C}
, the fraction
k|I|/(2\pi)
would be the expected number of points in
I
. The next lemma shows that the supremum
\textup{sup}_{I}V_{I}(\textbf{x})
is equivalent to
k\,d_{\infty}({\bf x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k})
. We note
\mathcal{I}
the set of all closed intervals
I\subset\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
.

Lemma B.18
.

For any integer
k\in[n]
and any vector
{\bf x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
of
k
distinct points of
\mathcal{C}
, we have

\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{I}({\bf x})|-4\leq\frac{k}{\pi}d_{\infty}({\bf x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k})\leq 2\,\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{I}({\bf x})|+4\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, to prove Lemma
B.17
, it is enough to show that, for
T=S
,
S^{\prime}
, and
\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}
, one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left||V_{I}({\bf x}^{*}_{T})|-\frac{|T|}{n}|V_{I}({\bf x}^{*})|\right|>C\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n^{3}}\ .

(47)

The next Lemma states a uniform concentration bound for
V_{I}
.

Lemma B.19
.

Consider any integer
n>4
and any integer
k<n
. Fix any
{\bf x}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}
. Sampling uniformly at random
k
coordinates of
{\bf x}
without replacement, we write
{\bf x}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
the resulting vector. Then, with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
, one has

\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left|\left|V_{I}({\bf x}^{(k)})\right|-\frac{k}{n}\left|V_{I}({\bf x})\right|\right|\leq 6\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

(48)

Since the marginal distributions of
S
,
S^{\prime}
, and
\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}
are uniform, we can apply Lemma
B.19
to
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
,
{\bf x}^{*}_{S^{\prime}}
, and
{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}\cap\overline{S}^{\prime}}
and the conclusion of the Lemma holds with probability higher than
1-3/n^{3}
, which is higher than
1-1/n^{2}
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
B.19
.

We start with a fixed interval
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Since
N_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})
is a hypergeometric random variable with parameters
(k,\frac{N_{I}(\textbf{x})}{n},n)
, we can invoke Hoeffding inequality (
82
) for hypergeometric distributions and get

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|N_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})-k\frac{N_{I}(\textbf{x})}{n}\right|\geq\sqrt{\frac{7k\log(n)}{2}}\right)\leq\frac{2}{n^{7}}\leq\frac{1}{n^{6}}\kern 5.0pt.

(49)

We combine (
49
) with

N_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})-k\frac{|I|}{2\pi}=\left(N_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})-k\frac{N_{I}(\textbf{x})}{n}\right)+\frac{k}{n}\left(N_{I}(\textbf{x})-n\frac{|I|}{2\pi}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

to conclude that

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left||V_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})|-\frac{k}{n}|V_{I}(\textbf{x})|\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{7n\log(n)}{2}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n^{6}}\kern 5.0pt.

(50)

In order to extend (
50
) to all intervals
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we use an
\varepsilon
-net approach with a subcollection
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{x})
of
\mathcal{I}
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{x})
be the collection of all intervals
I_{n}=[a_{n},b_{n}]
where
a_{n},b_{n}\in\{\underline{x}_{1}\ldots,\underline{x}_{n}\}\cup\mathcal{C}_{n}
, i.e.,
a_{n},b_{n}
are either coordinates of
x
or elements of the
n
-regular grid
\{2\pi i/n;\ \,i\in[n]\}
. We then apply (
50
) together with a union bound over all intervals
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{{x}})
. Since
|\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{{x}})|\leq(2n)^{2}\leq n^{3}
, we obtain

\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{{x}})}\left|\left|V_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})\right|-\frac{k}{n}\left|V_{I}(\textbf{x})\right|\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{7n\log(n)}{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

(51)

with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
.

To obtain (
51
) for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we observe that, for any
I\in\mathcal{I}
, there exists
I_{n}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\textbf{x})
such that

I=I^{(l)}\cup I_{n}\cup I^{(r)},

(52)

where
I^{(l)}
and
I^{(r)}
are two closed intervals of
I\setminus{I_{n}}
whose lengths are smaller than
2\pi/n
and that satisfy
N_{I^{(l)}}(\textbf{x})=N_{I^{(r)}}(\textbf{x})=0
. In particular, we have
N_{I^{(l)}}(\textbf{x}^{(r)})=N_{I^{(l)}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})=0
. We then deduce that

\displaystyle V_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=V_{I^{(l)}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})+V_{I_{n}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})+V_{I^{(r)}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=-k\frac{|I^{(l)}|}{2\pi}+V_{I_{n}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})-k\frac{|I^{(r)}|}{2\pi}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the same decomposition holds for
V_{I}(\textbf{x})
, we get

\left||V_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})|-\frac{k}{n}|V_{I}(\textbf{x})|\right|\leq 4+\left||V_{I_{n}}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})|-\frac{k}{n}|V_{I_{n}}(\textbf{x})|\right|

Together with (
51
), we obtain

\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left|\left|V_{I}(\textbf{x}^{(k)})\right|-\frac{k}{n}\left|V_{I}(\textbf{x})\right|\right|\leq 4+\sqrt{\frac{7n\log(n)}{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

with probability higher than
1-1/n^{3}
. Lemma
B.19
is proved
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.18
.

We first prove the upper bound

kd_{\infty}({\bf x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k})\leq 2\pi[\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{I}({\bf x})|+2]\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that for a vector
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
, we say that
{\bf x}
is ordered, if these points are consecutive when one walks on the sphere with the trigonometric direction. Without loss of generality and for ease of exposition, we assume that the identity permutation is a latent order, that is
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
is ordered.

We define
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}=(x_{1}^{**},\ldots,x_{k}^{**})
a vector of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k}
as follows. The first point
x_{1}^{**}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
is a closest point to
x_{1}^{*}
with respect to
d
and the other points
x_{j+1}^{**}
are elements of
\mathcal{C}_{k}
with arguments

\underline{x}_{j+1}^{**}=\underline{x}_{1}^{**}+j\frac{2\pi}{k}\ \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2\pi)\,,\quad\quad\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,k-1\kern 5.0pt.

Fix any
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
and consider the intervals
I_{i}=[\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{i}]
and
I^{\prime}_{i}=[\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{i}^{**}]
. We have

d(x_{i},x_{i}^{**})\leq\big||I_{i}|-|I^{\prime}_{i}|\big|\kern 5.0pt.

(53)

Observe that
N_{I_{i}}(\textbf{x})=i
since
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are ordered and all distinct. Hence,

\left|\frac{2\pi i}{k}-|I_{i}|\right|=\left|\frac{2\pi N_{I_{i}}}{k}-|I_{i}|\right|=2\pi\frac{|V_{I_{i}}(\textbf{x})|}{k}\leq 2\pi\,\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,\frac{|V_{I}(\textbf{x})|}{k}\kern 5.0pt.

(54)

Besides, we know that the length of
I^{\prime}_{i}
is equal to
\big|[\underline{x}_{1}^{**},\underline{x}_{i}^{**}]\big|
up to an additional term
d(x_{1},x_{1}^{**})
, that is

\left||I^{\prime}_{i}|-\big|[\underline{x}_{1}^{**},\underline{x}_{i}^{**}]\big|\right|\leq d(x_{1},x_{1}^{**})\kern 5.0pt.

By construction of the
x^{**}_{j}
’s, we have
d(x_{1},x_{1}^{**})\leq 2\pi/k
and
\big|[\underline{x}_{1}^{**},\underline{x}_{i}^{**}]\big|=2\pi(i-1)/k
. Hence,
we obtain
k||I^{\prime}_{i}|-2\pi i|\leq 4\pi
.
We then deduce from (
54
) and the triangular inequality that
k\big||I_{i}|-|I^{\prime}_{i}|\big|\leq 2\pi\,\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{I}|+4\pi
. Coming back to (
53
), taking the supremum over all
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
, and noting that
d(x_{1},x_{1}^{**})\leq 2\pi/k
leads us to

d_{\infty}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{**})\leq\frac{2\pi}{k}\left[\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{i}|+2\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\bf x}^{**}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k}
. Finally, we take the minimum over
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k}
to get the desired bound.

We now turn to the lower bound
kd_{\infty}({\bf x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k})\geq\pi[\underset{I\in\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,|V_{I}({\bf x})|-4]
.
Consider any such interval
I
and
\mathbf{x}^{**}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{k}
. Since the entries of
\mathbf{x}^{**}
are regularly spaced on
\mathcal{C}
, it follows that

|N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})-k|I|/(2\pi)|\leq 1
so that
|V_{I}(\mathbf{x})|\leq|N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})|+1
.
Now, assume that
N_{I}(\mathbf{x})>N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})
. We claim that
