\sup_{j:\,x_{j}\in I}|x_{j}-x_{j}^{**}|\geq\frac{\pi}{k}[|N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})|-3]
. Otherwise, the set of
x_{j}^{**}
with
j
satisfying
x_{j}\in I
is included in an interval of size

|I|+\frac{2\pi}{k}[N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})-3]\leq\frac{2\pi}{k}[N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-2]\kern 5.0pt.

This contradicts the fact that this set of equi-spaced points has size
N_{I}(\mathbf{x})
. If
N_{I}(\mathbf{x})<N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})
, we simply consider the complement
3
3

3

Although
\overline{I}
is an open interval, the arguments are still valid.
interval
\overline{I}
that satisfies
N_{\overline{I}}(\mathbf{x})=k-N_{I}(\mathbf{x})
and
N_{\overline{I}}(\mathbf{x}^{**})=k-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})
to conclude that

\sup_{j:\,x_{j}\in\overline{I}}|x_{j}-x_{j}^{**}|\geq\frac{\pi}{k}\left[|N_{\overline{I}}(\mathbf{x})-N_{\overline{I}}(\mathbf{x}^{**})|-3\right]=\frac{\pi}{k}\left[|N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})|-3\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Putting everything together, we have shown that

d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{**})\geq\frac{\pi}{k}\left[|N_{I}(\mathbf{x})-N_{I}(\mathbf{x}^{**})|-3\right]\geq\frac{\pi}{k}\left[|V_{I}(\mathbf{x})|-4\right]\ .

Taking the infimum over
\mathbf{x}^{**}
and the supremum over
I
leads to the desired result.

∎

B.5
Proof of Corollary
3.3

Theorem
3.2
ensures that, conditionally to
{\bf x}^{*}
,

\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}},Q{\bf x}^{*})\leq C^{\prime}_{lLe}\left(d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with probability at least
1-5/n^{2}
. Thus, it suffices to show that, with probability at least
1-2/n^{2}
, one has

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

for some
C>0
.
We shall rely on Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz (DKW) inequality. Indeed, the arguments
\underline{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\underline{x}_{n}^{*}
are independent and uniformly distributed on
[0,2\pi)
. Besides, any interval
I
of the torus
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
can be represented as a union of at most two intervals of
[0,2\pi)
. For any interval
I
, we denote
|I|
its length and
N_{I}({\bf x}^{*})
the number of points
x^{*}_{i}
whose argument lies in
I
. Then, we deduce from DKW inequality that, for any
t>0
,

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left(\underset{I\subset\,\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)}{\textup{sup}}\left|\frac{N_{I}({\bf x}^{*})}{n}-\frac{|I|}{2\pi}\right|>4t\right)\leq 2e^{-2nt^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

We then choose
t=\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
to obtain

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left(\underset{I\subset\,\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)}{\textup{sup}}\left|\frac{N_{I}({\bf x}^{*})}{n}-\frac{|I|}{2\pi}\right|>4\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\leq\frac{2}{n^{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

Besides, by Lemma
B.18
, we know that the quantity
V_{I}({\bf x}^{*})=N_{I}({\bf x}^{*})-\frac{n|I|}{2\pi}
introduced in (
46
) satisfies

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq C\left(\underset{I\subset\,\mathcal{I}}{\textup{sup}}\,\frac{|V_{I}({\bf x}^{*})|}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathcal{I}
stands for the set of interval on the torus
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
. The last two displays lead to the desired result.

\square

C
Proof of the identifiability results and minimax lower bound

C.1
Proof of Proposition
2.2

For simplicity, we assume that
n/8
is an integer in the rest of the example and we write
n=8n_{1}
. The construction of
f^{\prime}
mainly amounts to contracting the function
f
in some regions and dilating it in other regions which allows to contracting and dilating the positions
{\bf x}
.

Consider a partition of the latent space
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}
in three arcs
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{x}_{n_{1}}]=(0,\pi/4]
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}=(\underline{x}_{n_{1}},\underline{x}_{4n_{1}}]=(\pi/4,\pi]
and
\mathcal{C}_{3}=(\underline{x}_{4n_{1}},\underline{x}_{n}]=(\pi,2\pi]
. For
x
and
y
belonging
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, define
f^{\prime}_{1}(x,y)
by
f^{\prime}_{1}(x,y)=1-d(x,y)/\pi
. For
k=1,\ldots,2n_{1}
, define
x^{\prime}_{k}=e^{\iota k\pi/n}
and let
x^{\prime}_{n}=x_{n}=1
. In other words, we contract the positions
x_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,2n_{1}
. Although we have not yet completely defined
{\bf x}^{\prime}
, we already can certify that
\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}({\bf x},Q{\bf x}^{\prime})\geq\pi/8
. Besides, we have
f^{\prime}_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(x_{i},x_{j})
for all
i,j\in[2n_{1}]\cup\{n\}
.

For
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, we define
f^{\prime}_{2}(x,y)
by
f^{\prime}_{2}(x,y)=1-d(x,y)/(3\pi)
. For
k=1,\ldots,2n_{1}
, we set
x^{\prime}_{k+2n_{1}}=e^{\iota\pi/4}e^{\iota k3\pi/n}
. Again, observe that
f^{\prime}_{2}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(x_{i},x_{j})
for all integers
i,j
in
[2n_{1}+1,4n_{1}]
. Finally, for
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{C}_{3}
, set
f^{\prime}_{3}(x,y)=f(x,y)
, and let
x^{\prime}_{k}=x_{k}
for all integers
k=4n_{1}+1,\ldots,n-1
. Obviously, we have
f^{\prime}_{3}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(x_{i},x_{j})
for all integers
i,j\in[4n_{1},n]
.

It remains to deal with the situations where the pairs of points lie in different parts of the partition
\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}
. In the case where
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
y\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
, define
f^{\prime}_{1-2}(x,y)=f^{\prime}_{1}(x,e^{\iota\pi/4})+f^{\prime}_{2}(e^{\iota\pi/4},y)-1.
For all integers
i\in[0,2n_{1}]
and
j\in[2n_{1},4n_{1}]
, we have already seen that
f^{\prime}_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i},e^{\iota\pi/4})=f(x_{i},e^{\iota\pi/2})
and
f^{\prime}_{1}(e^{\iota\pi/4},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(e^{\iota\pi/2},x_{j})
. Hence
f^{\prime}_{1-2}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(x_{i},e^{\iota\pi/2})+f(e^{\iota\pi/2},x_{j})-1=f(x_{i},x_{j})
.

In the case where
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
y\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
, define
f^{\prime}_{1-3}(x,y)=f^{\prime}_{1}(x,e^{\iota 0})+f^{\prime}_{3}(e^{\iota 0},y)-1
if the length of the arc
[x,e^{\iota 0}]\cup(e^{\iota 0},y]
is less than
\pi
; otherwise, set
f^{\prime}_{1-3}(x,y)=f^{\prime}_{1}(x,e^{\iota\pi/4})+f^{\prime}_{2}(e^{\iota\pi/4},e^{\iota\pi})+f^{\prime}_{3}(e^{\iota\pi},y)-2.
Since
f
admits similar decompositions, one can deduce from the above that
f^{\prime}_{1-3}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})=f(x_{i},x_{j})
for all
i\in[1,2n_{1}]
and
j\in[4n_{1}+1,n]
.

The remaining cases can be handled in the same manner. Finally, we define the symmetric function
f^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
relying on
f^{\prime}_{1}
,
f^{\prime}_{2},

f^{\prime}_{3}
,
f^{\prime}_{1-2}
,
f^{\prime}_{1-3}
, and
f^{\prime}_{2-3}
. Then, we can readily check that
f\in\mathcal{BL}[(3\pi)^{-1},\pi^{-1},0]
and that
f(x_{i},x_{j})=f^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{j})
for all
i,j\in[n]
. As a consequence,
({\bf x}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
belongs to
\mathcal{R}[F,(3\pi)^{-1},\pi^{-1},0]
. One easily check that
{\bf x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{ev}
and the result follows.

\square

C.2
Proof of (
9
) in Proposition
2.3

We show in the paragraph below that (
9
) is a consequence of the proof of Theorem
3.2
in the noiseless case (
E=0
), after application of the triangular inequality. Indeed, since the noise is equal to zero, the conclusion of Theorem
3.2
is deterministic (and not with high probability anymore), so it can be used to prove deterministic inequalities such as (
9
). By doing so, we establish (
9
) via our localization algorithm (Theorem
3.2
), though (
9
) is an approximation result (independent of any algorithm) which could be proved directly.

Consider any two representations
(\mathbf{x},f)
and
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
in
R[F,c_{l},c_{L},c_{e}]
and apply our Localize-and-Refine procedure to noiseless observations
A=F
. The conclusion of Theorem
3.2
applies to both
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, so that we have

\displaystyle\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}},Q\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{lLe}\left(d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\log(n)\over n}\right)

\displaystyle\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}},Q\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{lLe}\left(d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\log(n)\over n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, it follows from the triangular inequality that

\displaystyle\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x},Q\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{Q_{1},\ Q_{2}\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(Q_{1}\mathbf{x},Q_{2}\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{lLe}\left(d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+2\sqrt{\log(n)\over n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

C.3
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We establish the lower bound
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
in the particular setting where the observations
A_{ij}
are independent Bernoulli random variables of parameters
F_{ij}=f_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})
, for the specific function

f_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})=(3/4)-d(x_{i},x_{j})/(4\pi),

(55)

with
\textup{{\bf x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}.
The corresponding probability distribution is denoted by
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}},f_{0})}
.

This minimax lower bound is based on Fano’s method as stated below. For two configuration
{\bf x}
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
, we denote the Kullback-Leibler divergence of
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}},f_{0})}
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}}^{\prime},f_{0})}
by
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}}^{\prime},f_{0})})
. Besides, we quantify the quasi-metric
\rho({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}({\bf x},Q{\bf x}^{\prime})
. Given a radius
\delta>0
and a subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
, the packing number
\mathcal{M}(\delta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\rho)
is defined as the largest number of points in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
that are at quasi-distance
\rho
at least
\delta
away from each other. Below, we state a specific version of Fano’s lemma.

Lemma C.1

(from
[
41
]
)
.

Consider any subset
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. Define the Kullback-Leibler diameter of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
by

d_{KL}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\underset{{\textup{{\bf x}}},{\textup{{\bf x}}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}{\textup{sup}}KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\textup{{\bf x}}}^{\prime},f_{0})})\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for any estimator
\hat{\textup{{\bf x}}}
and for any
\delta>0
, we have

\underset{\textup{{\bf x}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}{\textup{sup}}\quad\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{(\textup{{\bf x}},f_{0})}\left[\rho(\hat{{\textup{{\bf x}}}},{\textup{{\bf x}}})\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{d_{KL}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})+\log(2)}{\log\mathcal{M}(\delta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\rho)}\kern 5.0pt.

In view of the above proposition, we mainly have to choose a suitable subset
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, control its Kullback diameter, and get a sharp lower bound of its packing number. The main difficulty stems from the fact that the loss function
\rho({\bf x},{\bf y})=\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}({\bf x},Q{\bf y})
is a minimum over a collection of orthogonal transformations. It is therefore challenging to derive a tight lower bound for this loss.

Let
k:=C^{\prime}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
, for a small enough constant
C^{\prime}\in(0,1]
that will be set later. Define
n/2
vectors
{\bf x}^{(s)}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
,
s=1,\ldots,n/2
, as follows. For each
s\in[n/2]
, we define
x_{j}^{(s)}
by its argument
\underline{x}^{(s)}_{j}

\displaystyle\underline{x}^{(s)}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi j}{n}\ ,\qquad\forall j\in[n]\setminus{\{s,s+k\}}\ ,\underline{x}^{(s)}_{s}=\frac{2\pi(s+k)}{n},\quad\underline{x}^{(s)}_{s+k}=\frac{2\pi s}{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Each vector of arguments
\underline{\bf x}^{(s)}
is therefore equal to the vector
(2\pi j/n)_{j\in[n]}
up to an exchange of the positions
2\pi s/n
and
2\pi(s+k)/n
. This collection of
n/2
vectors is denoted by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{{\bf x}^{(1)},\ldots,{\bf x}^{(n/2)}\}
. Obviously
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
, and one can readily checks that

\rho({\bf x}^{(t)},{\bf x}^{(s)})\geq\frac{\pi k}{n}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad\forall s,t\in\left[\frac{n}{2}\right],\,s\neq t\kern 5.0pt,

(56)

which in turn ensures that the packing number
\mathcal{M}(\delta_{n},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\rho)
of radius
\delta_{n}:=\pi k/n
satisfies

\mathcal{M}(\delta_{n},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\rho)\geq n/2
.
To upper bound the KL diameter of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we use the following claim whose proof is postponed to the end of the section.

Claim C.2
.

For any
{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{n},
we have

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x}^{\prime},f_{0})})\leq 8\sum_{i,j}(f_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})-f_{0}(x_{i}^{\prime},x_{j}^{\prime}))^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Together with the definition (
55
) of
f_{0}
, we get

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x}^{(t)},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x}^{(s)},f_{0})})\leq Cn\delta_{n}^{2}\leq C(C^{\prime})^{2}\log(n)\kern 5.0pt,

for some numerical constant
C
. Then, choosing the constant
C^{\prime}
in the definition of
k
such that
C^{\prime}=(2\sqrt{C})^{-1}
leads to
d_{KL}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\leq\log(n)/4.

Applying Lemma
C.1
to this set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we arrive at

\inf_{\hat{\textup{{\bf x}}}}\underset{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}{\textup{sup}}\quad\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x},f_{0})}\left[\rho(\hat{\textup{{\bf x}}},\textup{{\bf x}})\geq\frac{\delta_{n}}{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{\log(n)/4+\log(2)}{\log(n/2)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\kern 5.0pt,

as soon as
n
is large enough. Theorem
5.1
is proved.
\square

Proof of Claim
C.2
.

By definition of the Kullback-Leibler divergence, and
F_{ij}=f_{0}(x_{i},x_{j})
and
F^{\prime}_{ij}

=

f_{0}(x_{i}^{\prime},x_{j}^{\prime})
, we have

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x}^{\prime},f_{0})})=\sum_{i<j}F_{ij}\log\frac{F_{ij}}{F^{\prime}_{ij}}+(1-F_{ij})\log\frac{1-F_{ij}}{1-F^{\prime}_{ij}}\kern 5.0pt,

and since
\log(t)\leq t-1
for all
t>0
, it follows that

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x},f_{0})}\|\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{({\bf x}^{\prime},f_{0})})\leq\sum_{ij}\frac{(F_{ij}-F^{\prime}_{ij})^{2}}{F^{\prime}_{ij}(1-F^{\prime}_{ij})}\leq 8\sum_{i,j}(F_{ij}-F^{\prime}_{ij})^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
1/4\leq F^{\prime}_{ij}\leq 3/4
.
∎

D
Proof for the spectral method

D.1
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Recall that the Spectral Localization (LS) algorithm is applied to the data matrix
A_{SS}
, where
S
is a subset of indices of
[n]
, with a cardinal number
|S|=n_{0}=n/4
. We can assume that
S=\{1,\ldots,n_{0}\}
for the ease of exposition. Vanilla Spectral Localization in LS algorithm returns
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}:=(\hat{x}_{1}^{\textup{VSA}},\ldots,\hat{x}_{n_{0}}^{\textup{VSA}})
with
\hat{x}_{i}^{\textup{VSA}}:=(\hat{x}_{ij}^{\textup{VSA}})_{j\in[2]}=\sqrt{\frac{n_{0}}{2}}(\hat{u}_{i},\hat{v}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
i=1,\ldots,n_{0}
.
We denote by
\hat{\lambda}_{0}^{(S)}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{n_{0}-1}^{(S)}
the eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix
A_{SS}
.

Note that the position estimates
\hat{x}_{i}^{\textup{VSA}}
do not lie on the unit sphere
\mathcal{C}
. As a consequence, the quantity
d(\hat{x}_{i}^{\textup{VSA}},x^{*}_{i})
is not defined, and we will use the distance
\|\hat{\bf x}^{\textup{VSA}}_{S}-{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}|\hat{x}_{ij}^{\textup{VSA}}-x^{*}_{ij}|
where
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
is interpreted as a
2\times n_{0}
matrix. Besides, since
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
can only be recovered up to orthogonal transformations, we consider the loss
\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}
where
Q
is interpreted as
2\times 2
orthogonal matrix.

Let
\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n_{0}-1}^{*(S)}
denote the eigenvalues of the signal matrix
F_{SS}:=[f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*}))]_{i,j\in S}
. We denote by
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}:=\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)}
and
\Delta_{2}^{(S)}:=\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)}
the two relevant spectral gaps.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
n_{0}\geq 4
, and
f
be a geometric function as defined in (
23
), such that
f
belongs to
\mathcal{BL}[c_{l},c_{L},c_{e}]
. Let
c_{a}>0
be any positive constant. Assume that the latent positions
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
fulfill the following inequality

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq c_{a}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

(57)

Then, with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
, the spectral estimator
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}
satisfies

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C_{lLea}\,\frac{n\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\left(\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\right)\vee 1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proposition
D.1
is based on the fact that the signal matrix
F_{SS}
is well approximated by a circulant and circular-R matrix, which benefits from nice spectral properties. See Appendix
D.4
for a proof.

Assumption (
24
) of the theorem states that
d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq c_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. Since Lemma
B.17
ensures that
d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq C[d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})+\sqrt{\log(n)/n}]
with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
, we get the bound

d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq C_{a}\kern 5.0pt\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt,

(58)

which holds with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
.

The
\ell^{1}
-type localization bound in (
D.1
) depends on the spectral gap
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}
of the signal matrix
F_{SS}
. We combine the next lemma with the assumption
\Delta_{1}\wedge\Delta_{2}\geq c_{b}n
of the theorem to get the following lower bound

\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\geq c_{b}n/4\kern 5.0pt,

(59)

which holds with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
, as soon as
n\geq C_{abL}
.

Lemma D.2
.

If
\Delta_{1}\wedge\Delta_{2}\geq c_{b}n
for some constant
c_{b}>0
, then with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
we have
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\geq c_{b}n/4
for all
n\geq C_{ab}
where
C_{abL}
is a positive quantity depending only on
c_{a}
,
c_{b}
, and
c_{L}
.

Hence, By (
59
) and (
58
) the conditions of Proposition
D.1
are satisfied. In summary, there exists an event of probability higher than
1-3/n^{2}
such that

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C_{lLeab}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt,

(60)

as soon as
n\geq C_{abL}
. For
n\leq C_{abL}
, the bound (
60
) trivially holds provided that we adjust the constant
C_{lLeab}
if necessary.

Since
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}
does not lie in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}\subset\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
, we cannot directly plug it into the local refinement step defined by (
16
). Accordingly, the Uniform Approximation (UA) in LS algorithm, projects
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}
onto
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}\subset\mathcal{C}^{{n_{0}}}
. The UA outputs a vector
\tilde{\bf x}_{S}^{(1)}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
that is close to the input
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}
– see Lemma
D.3
below.

Lemma
D.3
actually gives a more general result that holds for any input in
\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
given to UA. For clarity, we write below the UA procedure in full generality.

Uniform Approximation (UA) in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}

Input:

\mathbf{x}_{S}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{0}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
.

1.

Set
z_{i}=x_{i}/\|x_{i}\|_{2}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n_{0}
.

2.

Pick any permutation
\sigma
such that
z_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,z_{\sigma(n_{0})}
is in trigonometric order.

3.

Set
\displaystyle{\tilde{x}_{\sigma(i)}=e^{\iota{2\pi(\widehat{k}+i)\over n_{0}}},\quad\textrm{where}\quad\widehat{k}\in\argmin_{k\in[n_{0}]}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\left\|e^{\iota{2\pi(k+i)\over n_{0}}}-z_{\sigma(i)}\right\|_{1}}.

Output:

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
. For any input
{\bf x}_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
, UA returns a vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
such that

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\kern 5.0pt.

By (
58
-
60
) and Lemma
D.3
, the projection
\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}^{(1)}
satisfies

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}^{(1)}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\leq C_{lLeab}\,\sqrt{n\log(n)}

(61)

with probability higher than
1-3/n^{2}
.

Finally, we plug
\widetilde{\bf x}_{S}^{(1)}
in the criterion (
16
) to localize the remaining points. In other words, we compute, for
i\in\overline{S}
,

\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}=\underset{z\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{0}}}{\textup{argmin}}\,\langle A_{i,S},D(z,\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}^{(1)})\rangle\kern 5.0pt,

(62)

and get the position estimates
\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}}^{(2)}=[\hat{x}_{i}^{(2)}]_{i\in S}
.
As a direct consequence of (
61
) and Propositions
3.5
(and the equivalence between the
\ell^{1}
norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the distance
d_{1}
in the sphere
\mathcal{C}
), we arrive at the following uniform bound

\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}(\hat{{\bf x}}_{\overline{S}}^{(2)},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{\overline{S}})\leq C_{lLeab}\,\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

which holds with probability higher than
1-4/n^{2}
.

As in section
3.4
, we finally rely on a cross-validation scheme to estimate and realign all the positions. This straightforwardly allows us to uniformly localize, with probability higher than
1-9/n^{2}
, all positions
{\bf x}^{*}_{i}
within an error of the order of
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
\square

D.2
Proof of Lemma
D.2

Recall that
S=[n_{0}]
for the ease of exposition. In order too show that the spectrums of
F
and
F_{SS}:=[f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})]_{i,j\in S}
are linked together, we introduce an intermediate matrix
F^{(4)}:=[f(x_{i}^{*(4)},x_{j}^{*(4)})]_{i,j\in[n]}
based on the vector
{\bf x}^{*(4)}_{S}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}
with coordinates
x_{i}^{*(4)}=x_{\lceil i/4\rceil}^{*}
for
i\in[n_{0}]
(where
\lceil\cdot\rceil
denotes the ceiling function).
In other words, we replicate
4
-times each coordinate of the vector
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
to get the vector
{\bf x}^{*(4)}_{S}
of size
n
which is close to
{\bf x}^{*}
.

Let us show first that the spectrums of
F_{SS}
and
F^{(4)}
are almost the same. By construction of
F^{(4)}
, each of the
n_{0}
eigenvectors of
F_{SS}
can be transformed into an eigenvector of
F^{(4)}
, by replicating
4
-times the coordinates of these vectors. Besides, the rank of
F^{(4)}
is the same as that of
F_{SS}
. We deduce that all non-zero eigenvalues of
F^{(4)}
are eigenvalues of
2F_{SS}
. Formally, denoting the eigenvalues of
F^{(4)}
by
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n-1}^{\prime}
, and recalling that the eigenvalues of
F_{SS}
are denoted by
\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n_{0}-1}^{*(S)}
, we have

2\lambda_{i}^{*(S)}=\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\ ,\quad\quad\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,n_{0}\kern 5.0pt.

(63)

We then show that the spectrums of
F^{(4)}
and
F
are close. By (
58
) there is a probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\leq C_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. Hence, one can readily check that

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*(4)},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*(4)},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+2\pi/n_{0}\leq C^{\prime}_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, Assumption (
24
) of the theorem ensures that

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq c_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, both
{\bf x}^{*(4)}
and
{\bf x}^{*}
are close to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
. Since (any) two elements of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
are equal up to a permutation of their indices
[n]
, we deduce that there exists a permutation
\sigma
of
[n]
satisfying
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*(4)},{\bf x}_{\sigma}^{*})\leq C_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. Combining this with the bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
) we deduce that

\|F^{(4)}-F_{\sigma}\|_{2}\leq C_{a}c_{L}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

We are now ready to control the difference between the spectrums of
F
and
F^{(4)}
. Recalling that
\lambda_{0}^{*}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n-1}^{*}
denote the eigenvalues of
F
, and since
F_{\sigma}
has the same eigenvalues as
F
, it follows from Weyl’s inequality (see e.g.
[
39
, page 45]
) that

|\lambda_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}^{*}|\leq\|F^{(4)}-F_{\sigma}\|_{op}\leq\|F^{(4)}-F_{\sigma}\|_{2}\leq C_{aL}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\ ,

(64)

for all
i=0,\ldots,n-1
, and some constant
C_{aL}
depending only on
c_{a}
and
c_{L}
.

Gathering (
63
-
64
), we conclude that the following implication holds. If
\Delta_{1}:=\lambda_{0}^{*}-\lambda_{1}^{*}
and
\Delta_{2}:=\lambda_{2}^{*}-\lambda_{3}^{*}
satisfies
\Delta\wedge\Delta_{2}\geq c_{b}n
, then
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}:=\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)}
and
\Delta_{2}^{(S)}:=\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)}
fulfills
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\geq\frac{c_{b}}{2}n-C\sqrt{n\log(n)}
which is larger than
c_{b}n/4
as soon as
n\geq n_{ab}
for
n_{ab}
the smallest integer satisfying
c_{b}n\geq 4C_{aL}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
.
\square

D.3
Proof of Lemma
D.3
(Uniform approximation)

Recall that for a vector
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n_{0}})\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
, we say that
{\bf x}
is ordered, if these points are consecutive when one walks on the circle following the trigonometric direction.

We introduce some notation. For any vector
{\bf v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n_{0}})\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
, denote
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf v})
all elements
{\bf u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n_{0}})
of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
such that, for all permutations
\sigma
making
v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(n_{0})}
ordered, the sequence
u_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,u_{\sigma(n_{0})}
is ordered. For any vector
{\bf v}\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
with distinct values, the set
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf v})
can be described by a single element
{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf v})
and all circular permutations of
{\bf u}
.

The bound of Lemma
D.3
trivially holds for
n_{0}\leq 3
. Henceforth, we assume that
n_{0}\geq 4
. The next lemma is a key element in the proof; it states that re-ordering two vectors is almost optimal for minimizing their
d_{1}
distance. This result is fairly classical for real vectors. Here, as the vectors
{\bf v}
and
{\bf u}
take their values on
\mathcal{C}
the proof is slightly more complicated.

Lemma D.4
.

Consider any
{\bf v}\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
. Provided that
n_{0}\geq 4
, we have

\min_{{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf v})}d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\sigma})\lesssim\min_{{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}}d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
S=\{1,\ldots,n_{0}\}
for the ease of exposition. We shall prove the following statement which implies Lemma
D.3
. For any
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
, any input
{\bf x}_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times n_{0}}
, and any
Q\in\mathcal{O}
, the vector
\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}
of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
fulfills

\|\widetilde{{\bf x}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1.

(65)

UA computes in step 1 the projection
{\bf z}_{S}=[x_{i}/\|x_{i}\|_{2}]_{i\in S}
of the input
{\bf x}_{S}
onto
\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
. Given the projection
{\bf z}_{S}
, UA picks in step 3 a vector
\tilde{\bf x}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf z}_{S})
that has the smallest
\ell^{1}
-error:

\tilde{\bf x}_{S}\in\underset{{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf z}_{S})}{\textup{argmin}}\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf u}\|_{1}.

It follows from these definitions and the equivalence between the distance
d
on
\mathcal{C}
and
\ell^{1}
-norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that

\|{\bf z}_{S}-\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}\|_{1}=\underset{{\bf v}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf z}_{S})}{\textup{min}}\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf v}\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{{\bf v}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf z}_{S})}{\textup{min}}d_{1}({\bf z}_{S},{\bf v})\kern 5.0pt.

Gathering this bound with Lemma
D.4
, we derive that

\|{\bf z}_{S}-\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}}{\textup{min}}d_{1}({\bf z}_{S},{\bf u})\lesssim\underset{{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}}{\textup{min}}\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf u}\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

As a consequence, it suffices to exhibit some
u\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
such that its
\ell^{1}
distance to the projection
{\bf z}_{S}
is small. This is precisely the purpose of the next lemma.

Lemma D.5
.

Consider any matrix
Q\in\mathcal{O}
. There exists
{\bf y}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
such that

\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf y}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude that

\|{\bf z}_{S}-\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf y}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\kern 5.0pt.

By triangular inequality, we have

\|\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\leq\|\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}-{\bf z}_{S}\|_{1}+\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

The definition of a projection –and the equivalence between the
\ell^{1}
-norm and the euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
– ensure that

\|{\bf z}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt,

since
{\bf z}_{S}
is the projection of
{\bf x}_{S}
on
\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
and
Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
is an element of
\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
.
The last three displays allow us to conclude that

\|\tilde{{\bf x}}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\kern 5.0pt,

which gives (
65
) using the triangle inequality again.
\square

D.3.1
Proofs of Lemma
D.5

Let
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
be a closest approximation of
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, that is, such that
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},{\bf x}^{**}_{S})=d_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})
.
The triangular inequality gives

\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf z}_{S}\|_{1}\leq\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}+\|{\bf x}_{S}-{\bf z}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality comes from the definition of a projection and the equivalence between the
\ell^{1}
-norm and the euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By the triangular inequality again, we get

\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\leq\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+\|Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}-{\bf x}_{S}\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

An orthogonal transformation preserves the distances.

\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}=\|{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim d_{1}({\bf x}^{**}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\leq nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\kern 5.0pt,

where we use again the equivalence between the distance
d
in
\mathcal{C}
and the
\ell^{1}
-norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Putting everything together, we conclude that

\|Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-{\bf z}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})\kern 5.0pt.

Although
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, this is not necessarily the case for
Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}
. Nevertheless, it is easy to check that there exists some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
such that
Q^{\prime}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
and
\|Q^{\prime}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim 1
. Setting

{\bf y}:=Q^{\prime}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, then we see that

\|{\bf y}-{\bf z}_{S}\|_{1}\lesssim\|{\bf x}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}+nd_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}})+1\kern 5.0pt,

(66)

which concludes the proof.

\square

D.3.2
Proof of Lemma
D.4

Fix any vector
{\bf v}\in\mathcal{C}^{n_{0}}
and any
{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
. We shall prove that

\min_{{\bf w}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}({\bf v})}d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf w})\lesssim d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\tau
be a permutation ordering the coordinates of
{\bf v}
on the unit sphere, meaning that
v_{\tau(1)},\ldots v_{\tau(n_{0})}
is ordered. For simplicity and without loss of generality, assume that
\tau
is the identity. Since
{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
, it there suffices to prove the existence of a permutation
\sigma
of
[n_{0}]
such that
u_{\sigma}
is ordered and

d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\sigma})\lesssim d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})

Define the set of ’bad’ indices
\mathcal{B}=\{i:d(u_{i},v_{i})\geq\pi/16\}
. If the cardinal of
\mathcal{B}
is larger than
n_{0}/2
, then
d_{1}({\bf u},{\bf v})\geq n_{0}\pi/32
and any vector
{\bf u}_{\sigma}
satisfies

d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\sigma})\leq n_{0}\pi\leq 32d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})
. Hence, we assume henceforth that
|\mathcal{B}|\leq n_{0}/2
.
First, we focus on the set of ’good’ indices
\mathcal{G}=[n_{0}]\setminus\mathcal{B}
. We establish the following claim at the end of the proof.

Claim D.6
.

There exists a permutation
\sigma
of
\mathcal{G}
such that the sequence
(u_{\sigma(j)})
with
j\in\mathcal{G}
is ordered and

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}d(u_{\sigma(i)},v_{i})\leq\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}d(u_{i},v_{i})

Hence, it is possible to order the restriction of
u
to
\mathcal{G}
without increasing the sum of the distances. It remains to transform
\sigma
into a permutation of
[n_{0}]
. We iteratively add elements of
\mathcal{B}
into
\sigma
. Consider any
i\in\mathcal{B}
. Let
k
and
l
be the two consecutive (modulo
n_{0}
) elements of
\mathcal{G}
such that
\underline{u}_{i}
belongs to the interval
[\underline{u}_{\sigma(k)},\underline{u}_{\sigma(l)})
of the torus
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
. Let
r
and
s
be the two consecutive elements of
\mathcal{G}
such that
i\in(r,s)
(where we work modulo
n_{0}
). Then, we define the permutation
\sigma^{\prime}
of
(\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\})
as follows.

If
(r,s)=(k,l)
, then we take
\sigma^{\prime}(j)=\sigma(j)
if
j\in\mathcal{G}
and
\sigma^{\prime}(i)=i
. One readily checks that the sequence
(u_{\sigma^{\prime}(j)})
with
j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}
is ordered and that
\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}}d(u_{\sigma^{\prime}(j)},v_{j})\leq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}}d(u_{j},v_{j})
.

Otherwise, we set
\sigma^{\prime}(i)=\sigma(s)
and
\sigma^{\prime}(k)=i
. For
j\in\mathcal{G}
, let
\text{succ}_{\mathcal{G}}(j)
be the successor of
j\in\mathcal{G}
, that is the smallest index
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
which is larger than
j
(modulo
n_{0}
). For any
j\in\mathcal{G}
in the segment
[s,k)
, we set
\sigma^{\prime}(j)=\sigma(\text{succ}_{\mathcal{G}}(j))
. Besides, we set
\sigma^{\prime}(j)=\sigma(j)
for all
j\in\mathcal{G}
in the segment
[l,r]
. In other words, we have shifted all elements in the segment
[s,k]
to successfully include
i
in the permutation
\sigma^{\prime}
. It follows from this definition that the sequence
u_{\sigma^{\prime}(j)}
with
j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}
is ordered.
By the triangular inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}}d(u_{\sigma^{\prime}(j)},v_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cap[l,r]}d(u_{\sigma(j)},v_{j})+d(u_{\sigma(s)},v_{i})+d(u_{i},v_{k})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cap[s,k)}d(u_{\sigma(\text{succ}_{\mathcal{G}}(j))},v_{j})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\pi+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}d(u_{\sigma(j)},v_{j})+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cap[s,k)}d(u_{\sigma(j)},u_{\sigma(\text{succ}_{\mathcal{G}}(j))})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\pi+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}d(u_{\sigma(j)},v_{j})\leq 4\pi+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{i\}}d(u_{j},v_{j})\kern 5.0pt,

where we used in the third line that
\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cap[s,k)}d(u_{\sigma(j)},u_{\sigma(\text{succ}_{\mathcal{G}}(j))})\leq 2\pi
. Indeed, the sequence
(u_{\sigma(j)})_{j\in\mathcal{G}\cap[s,k)}
is ordered on the sphere and this sum is therefore equal to the length of the arc
[\underline{u}_{\sigma(s)},\underline{u}_{\sigma(k)}]
.

By a straightforward induction, we manage to build a permutation
\overline{\sigma}
on
[n_{0}]
such that
(u_{\overline{\sigma}(j)})_{j\in[n_{0}]}
is ordered and

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[n_{0}]}d(u_{\overline{\sigma}(j)},v_{j})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\pi\big|\big\{i\in[n_{0}]\,:\,d(u_{i},v_{i})\geq\frac{\pi}{16}\big\}\big|+\sum_{j\in[n_{0}]}d(u_{j},v_{j})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 65\sum_{j\in[n_{0}]}d(u_{j},v_{j})\ ,

where we used Markov’s inequality in the last line. We have shown the desired result.
\square

Proof of claim
D.6
.

Without loss of generality, we assume in the proof that
\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
so that we build a permutation
\sigma
of
[n_{0}]
. Since
\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
,
{\bf u}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
satisfies
d_{\infty}({\bf u},{\bf v})\leq\pi/16
. We shall iteratively build a permutation
\sigma
such that
{\bf u}_{\sigma}
is ordered. Let us first partition the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)
into three parts
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi)=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
where
\mathcal{D}_{s}=\left[(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{3},s\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)
for
s=1,2,3
.

For
s=1,2,3
, define
I_{s}=\{i:\underline{v}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{s}\}
. Since
d_{\infty}({\bf u},{\bf v})\leq\pi/16
, it follows that
\{\underline{u}_{i}:i\in I_{s}\}\subset\left[(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{3}-\frac{\pi}{16},s\frac{2\pi}{3}+\frac{\pi}{16}\right)=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s}
. Note that the diameter of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s}
is smaller
2\pi/3+\pi/8<\pi
.
We have the decomposition

d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})=\sum_{s=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in I_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

For
s=1,2,3
, let
\sigma_{s}
denote the permutation of
I_{s}
such that the sequence
u_{\sigma_{s}(i)}
is ordered when
i
is in
I_{s}
.
Since the diameter of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s}
is at most
\pi
, the sequence
\underline{u}_{\sigma_{s}(i)}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s}
is isometric to an increasing sequence of points in
[0,\pi]\subset\mathbb{R}
endowed with the absolute value distance. It goes the same for the ordered sequence
\underline{v}_{i}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s}
. Next, we use the following classical property.

Claim D.7
.

Let
l\geq 1
be an integer and
{\bf a},{\bf b}
be two monotonic vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
, that is,
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\ldots\leq a_{l}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}\leq\ldots\leq b_{l}.
Then, for any permutation
\tau
of the indices
\{1,\ldots,l\}
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{l}|a_{i}-b_{i}|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{l}|a_{i}-b_{\tau(i)}|\quad\text{ and }\quad\max(|a_{i}-b_{i}|)\leq\max(|a_{i}-b_{\tau(i)}|)\kern 5.0pt.

It follows that, for
s=1,2,3
, we have

\sum_{i\in I_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{\sigma_{s}(i)})\leq\sum_{i\in I_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\sigma
be the permutation such that
\sigma(i)=\sigma_{s}(i)
if
i\in I_{s}
. Obviously, we have
d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\sigma})\leq d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})
. Besides,
u_{\sigma}
is ordered except possibly at the indices
J_{s}=\{i:\underline{u}_{\sigma(i)}\in[(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{3}-\frac{\pi}{16};(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{3}+\frac{\pi}{16}]\}
with
s=1,2,3
. Since
\max_{i}d(u_{\sigma(i)},v_{i})\leq\frac{\pi}{16}
by the second part of the above claim, all
\underline{u}_{\sigma(i)}
and
\underline{v}_{i}
with
i\in J_{s}
belong to an interval of length smaller than
\pi
. Besides,

d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\sigma})=\sum_{j\notin(\cup_{s}J_{s})}d(v_{i},u_{\sigma_{s}(i)})+\sum_{s=1}^{3}\sum_{i\in J_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{\sigma(i)})\ .

Hence, we can build as previously partitions
\sigma^{\prime}_{s}
of
J_{s}
that make
u_{\sigma^{\prime}_{s}(\sigma(i))}
ordered on
J_{s}
and so that

\sum_{i\in J_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{\sigma^{\prime}_{s}(\sigma(i))})\leq\sum_{i\in J_{s}}d(v_{i},u_{\sigma(i)})\ .

Defining
\overline{\sigma}(i)=\sigma_{s}^{\prime}(\sigma(i))
if
i\in J_{s}
for
s=1,2,3
and
\overline{\sigma}(i)=\sigma(i)
otherwise, we conclude that
{\bf u}_{\overline{\sigma}}
is ordered and that
d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u}_{\overline{\sigma}})\leq d_{1}({\bf v},{\bf u})
.
∎

D.4
Proof of Proposition
D.1

Under the extra assumption that
f
is geometric, i.e.
f
satisfies (
23
), we will show that the estimation error of the spectral algorithm is bounded by
\frac{n\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\left(\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\right)\vee 1}
in
\ell^{1}
-type norm. The proof consists in approximating the signal
F_{SS}
by a circulant and circular-R matrix (Definition
D.8
) whose spectrum is known (Lemma
D.9
) and provides information on the latent positions
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
. The difference between the spectrums of
F_{SS}
and
A_{SS}
will be bounded using the Davis-Kahan perturbation bound.

D.4.1
Preliminaries: general facts on R-matrices

Let us start by introducing the notion of circulant matrix (see
[
23
,
33
]
).

Definition D.8
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
, a symmetric matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is circulant if there exists a vector
{\bf a}
of size
n
such that
M_{ij}=a_{|i-j|}
and

\displaystyle\forall k=1,\ldots,n-1,\qquad a_{k}=a_{n-k}.

Moreover,
M
is a circulant and circular R-matrix if the above holds and the sequence
(a_{j})_{0\leq j\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
is non-increasing.

The spectrum of circulant matrices is known –see
[
23
]
and the references therein, which allows to easily deduce the spectrum of symmetric circulant matrices, see Proposition C.4 from
[
33
]
. For clarity, we recall this result below –with a small correction on the first coordinate of the eigenvector
{\bf v}^{(m)}
.

Lemma D.9

(spectrum of symmetric circulant matrices)
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be any symmetric circulant matrix associated to a vector
{\bf a}
(as above).

•

For
n=2p+1,
the eigenvalues of
M
are equal to

\alpha_{m}=a_{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}\cos\left(j\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\qquad m=0,\ldots,p\kern 5.0pt,

where each
\alpha_{m}
, for
m=1,\ldots,p,
has multiplicity
2
and is associated with the two following eigenvectors

\displaystyle{\bf u}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=(1,\cos(2\pi m/n),\ldots,\cos((n-1)2\pi m/n))

(67)

\displaystyle{\bf v}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=(0,\sin(2\pi m/n),\ldots,\sin((n-1)2\pi m/n))\kern 5.0pt.

For
m=0
,
\alpha_{0}
has multiplicity 1 and is associated to
{\bf u}^{(0)}=(1,\ldots,1).

•

For
n=2p,

\alpha_{m}=a_{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}a_{j}\cos(j\frac{2\pi m}{n})+a_{p}\cos(\pi m)\kern 5.0pt,\qquad m=0,\ldots,p\kern 5.0pt,

where each
\alpha_{m}
, for
m=1,\ldots,p-1,
is associated with the two eigenvectors in (
67
). The eigenvalue
\alpha_{p}
is associated with
{\bf u}^{(p)}=(1,-1,\ldots,1,-1)
.
For
m=0
,
\alpha_{0}
has multiplicity 1 and is associated to
{\bf u}^{(0)}=(1,\ldots,1).

If the vector
{\bf a}
has nonnegative entries, then
\alpha_{0}
is obviously the largest eigenvalue. The next lemma ensures that, for circular R-matrices,
\alpha_{1}
is the second largest eigenvalue. Its proof can be found in
[
33
, Proposition C.5]
.

Lemma D.10

(second largest eigenvalue)
.

For any symmetric and circulant circular R-matrix, with nonnegative entries and eigenvalues
\{\alpha_{m}\}
for
m=0,\ldots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
(as defined in Lemma
D.9
), we have
\alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{j}
for all
j=2,\ldots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.

Remark
: if
a_{j}=g(j\frac{2\pi}{n})
, then the discrete Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}(g)
as defined in (
25
) satisfies
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}(g)=\alpha_{k}
, for all for
k=0,\ldots,p
. In addition,
\mathcal{F}_{n-k,n}(g)=\mathcal{F}_{k,n}(g)
for all
k=1,\ldots,p
.

D.4.2
Main Proof of Proposition
D.1

Recall that
S\subset\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
satisfies
|S|=n_{0}=n/4
. For the ease of exposition we assume that
S=\{1,2,\ldots,n_{0}\}
and we only consider the case where
n_{0}
is odd (the case of even
n_{0}
being similar). Thus, we write
n_{0}=2p+1
in the following.
If
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
, then the bound in Proposition
D.1
trivially holds

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\leq 2n_{0}\leq C_{lLea}\frac{n\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\left(\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\right)\vee 1}\kern 5.0pt.

We assume therefore that
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}\wedge\Delta_{2}^{(S)}\geq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
for a quantity
C_{lLea}
that will be set later. By definition of
\Delta_{1}^{(S)}
and
\Delta_{2}^{(S)}
, this means that

|\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)}|\wedge|\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)}|\geq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}.

(68)

Let
{\bf u}^{(1)}=(u^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,u^{(1)}_{n_{0}})
and
{\bf v}^{(1)}=(v^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,v^{(1)}_{n_{0}})
denote the eigenvectors of a circular and circulant
R
-matrix as described in Lemma
D.9
. For any matrix
M=(m_{ij})
, we write
\|M\|_{\infty}
its entry-wise
l_{\infty}
norm, that is
\|M\|_{\infty}=\textup{max}_{ij}\,m_{ij}
. Recall that
F_{SS}:=[f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})]_{i,j\in S}
.

Lemma D.11
.

There exist a permutation
\sigma
and a circulant circular R-matrix
R
with nonnegative entries such that the following inequality holds
\|F_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. Besides, the vector
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
defined by
x^{**}_{i}:=(u^{(1)}_{\sigma(i)},v_{\sigma(i)}^{(1)})
for
i=1,\ldots,n_{0}
satisfies

\min_{Q\in\mathcal{O}}d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{**}_{S},Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S})\leq C_{a}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

(69)

Denote
\lambda_{0}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n_{0}-1}
the eigenvalues of
R
. Lemmas
D.9
and
D.10
ensure that

\lambda_{0}=\alpha_{0}\geq\lambda_{1}=\alpha_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}=\alpha_{1}\geq\lambda_{j}\kern 5.0pt,

(70)

where
\lambda_{j}\in\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor}\}
for all
j=3,\ldots,n_{0}-1
.

Lemma
D.11
ensures that there exists a constant
C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}
depending only on
c_{l},c_{L},c_{e},c_{a}
such that
\|F_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
. Since
R_{\sigma}
has the same eigenvalues as
R
, it follows from Weyl’s inequality (see e.g.
[
39
, page 45]
) that

|\lambda_{i}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{i}|\leq\|F_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{op}\leq\|F_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\ ,

(71)

for all
i=0,\ldots,n_{0}-1
.

If the constant
C_{lLea}
in (
68
) is chosen as
4C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}
where
C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}
is introduced in (
71
), it follows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)})-(\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{0})-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)})

(72)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(C_{lLea}-2C_{lLea}^{\prime\prime})\sqrt{n\log(n)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{lLea}}{2}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt,

and similarly,

\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}\geq(\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)})-(\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{2})-(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)})\geq\frac{C_{lLea}}{2}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the eigenvectors
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf u}^{(1)}
and
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf v}^{(1)}
of
R
are orthonormal (see Lemma
D.12
below), the vectors
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf u}^{(1)}_{\sigma}
and
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf v}^{(1)}_{\sigma}
are orthonormal eigenvectors of
R_{\sigma}
, with the same eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\alpha_{1}
.

Lemma D.12
.

The vectors
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf u}^{(1)}
and
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf v}^{(1)}
are orthonormal.

Next, we state a variant of Davis-Kahan perturbation bound
[
42
, see Theorem 2]
.

Lemma D.13

(Davis-Kahan)
.

Let
M
,
\hat{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times n_{0}}
be two symmetric matrices, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n_{0}-1}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{0}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{n_{0}-1}
respectively. Fix
0\leq r\leq s\leq n_{0}-1
and assume that
(\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r})\wedge(\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1})>0,
where
\lambda_{-1}=\infty
and
\lambda_{n}=-\infty.
Let
d=s-r+1,
and let
\textup{{\bf V}}=({\bf v}_{r},{\bf v}_{r+1},\ldots,{\bf v}_{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times d}
and
\hat{\textup{{\bf V}}}=(\hat{\bf v}_{r},\hat{\bf v}_{r+1},\ldots,\hat{\bf v}_{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times d}
have orthonormal columns satisfying
M{\bf v}_{j}=\lambda_{j}{\bf v}_{j}
and
\hat{M}\hat{\bf v}_{j}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{\bf v}_{j}
for
j=r,r+1,\ldots,s
. Then, there exists an orthogonal matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that

\|\hat{\textup{{\bf V}}}Q-\textup{{\bf V}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{8d}\frac{\|\hat{M}-M\|_{op}}{(\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r})\wedge(\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1})}\kern 5.0pt.

The assumptions of Lemma
D.13
are therefore fulfilled for the orthonormal eigenvectors
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf u}^{(1)}_{\sigma}
and
(\sqrt{2/n_{0}}){\bf v}^{(1)}_{\sigma}
and the positive spectral gaps
(\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1})\wedge(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})>0.
Hence, for
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}
and
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}=({\bf u}^{(1)}_{\sigma},{\bf v}^{(1)}_{\sigma})^{T}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2\times n}
, Lemma
D.13
entails

\sqrt{\frac{2}{n_{0}}}\|Q\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\|A_{S,S}-R_{\sigma}\|_{op}}{(\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1})\wedge(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}

for some
Q\in\mathcal{O}.

It remains to control
\|A_{S,S}-R_{\sigma}\|_{op}
and the spectral gap. Since
A_{ii}=0
for all
i
(by convention), we introduce the matrix
F_{SS}^{(0)}
such that
F_{ii}^{(0)}=0
for all
i
, and
F_{ij}^{(0)}=F_{ij}
for all
i\neq j
.

\|A_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{op}\leq\|A_{SS}-F_{SS}^{(0)}\|_{op}+\|F_{SS}^{(0)}-F_{SS}\|_{op}+\|F_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

(73)

using the triangular inequality and
the fact that the operator norm is smaller than the Frobenius norm. The second term
\|F_{SS}^{(0)}-F_{SS}\|_{op}
is smaller than
1
since
F_{SS}-F_{SS}^{(0)}
is the diagonal matrix with diagonal coefficients
f(x^{*}_{i},x_{i}^{*})\leq 1
, the last inequality coming from
f\in\mathcal{BL}[c_{l},c_{L},c_{e}]
.
To control the operator norm of the noise matrix, we shall use the following result
[
40
, Corollary 4.4.8]
. See the same reference for the definition of sub-Gaussian norms
\|.\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

Lemma D.14

(norm of symmetric matrices with sub-gaussian entries)
.

Let
A
be an
n_{0}\times n_{0}
symmetric random matrix whose entries
A_{ij}
on and above the diagonal are independent mean-zero sub-gaussian random variables. Then, for any
t>0,
we have

\|A\|_{op}\leq CK(\sqrt{n}+t)

with probability at least
1-4e^{-t^{2}}.
Here
K=\textup{max}_{i,j}\|A_{i,j}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

Applying the above lemma with
t=C\sqrt{\log(n)}
(for a large enough numerical constant
C
) to the difference
A_{SS}-F_{SS}^{(0)}
, we obtain
\|A_{SS}-F_{SS}^{(0)}\|_{op}\lesssim\sqrt{n}
with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
.

Together with Lemma
D.11
and the bound (
73
), we deduce that
\|A_{SS}-R_{\sigma}\|_{op}\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
, so that

\|Q\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{2}\leq C_{lLea}\frac{n\sqrt{\log(n)}}{(\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1})\wedge(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we deduce from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\underset{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q^{\prime}{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{1}\leq\|Q\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C_{lLea}\frac{n\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{(\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1})\wedge(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}\kern 5.0pt.

The bounds (
68
) and (
71
) for
C_{lLea}=4C^{\prime\prime}_{lLea}
allow us to replace the above spectral gaps by
(\lambda_{0}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{1}^{*(S)})\wedge(\lambda_{2}^{*(S)}-\lambda_{3}^{*(S)})
.
Finally, by (
69
) and the equivalence between the distance
d
in
\mathcal{C}
and the
\ell^{1}
-norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have
\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|{\bf x}^{*}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{**}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C_{a}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
. Since
\lambda_{0}\leq n
(all the entries of
F_{SS}
belong to
[0,1]
), we then deduce from the triangular inequality that

\underset{Q\in\mathcal{O}}{\textup{min}}\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S}-Q{\bf x}^{*}_{S}\|_{1}\leq C^{\prime}_{lLea}\frac{n\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{(\lambda^{*(S)}_{0}-\lambda^{*(S)}_{1})\wedge(\lambda^{*(S)}_{2}-\lambda^{*(S)}_{3})}\kern 5.0pt.

Proposition
D.1
is proved.
\square

D.4.3
Proofs of technical lemmas

Proof of Lemma
D.11
.

In this proof, we replace the notation
F_{SS}
by
F_{({\bf x}^{*}_{S},f)}
for clarity.
Since the vector
{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
satisfies (
57
), there exists
{\bf x}_{S}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
such that the following inequality holds

d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*}_{S},{\bf x}_{S})\leq C_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt.

(74)

Combining this with the bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
), we get

\max_{ij\in[n_{0}]}|f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})-f(x_{i},x_{j})|\leq C_{Lea}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
, that is,

\|F_{({\bf x}^{*}_{S},f)}-F_{({\bf x}_{S},f)}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{Lea}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

(75)

for the matrices
F_{({\bf x}^{*}_{S},f)}:=[f(x_{i}^{*},x_{j}^{*})]_{i,j\in[n_{0}]}
and
F_{({\bf x}_{S},f)}:=[f(x_{i},x_{j})]_{i,j\in[n_{0}]}
.

Recall that
S=\{1,\ldots,n_{0}\}
for the ease of exposition. Let
\tau
be some permutation that orders
{\bf x}_{S}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{0}})
on the unit sphere, that is, such that
x_{\tau(1)},\ldots,x_{\tau(n_{0})}
is ordered. Then, one can observe that the matrix
F_{({\bf x}_{S},f),\tau}
is symmetric circulant since
f
is a symmetric function which satisfies the geometric condition (
23
) with respect to the geodesic distance
d
on the unit sphere
\mathcal{C}
.

The matrix
F_{({\bf x}_{S},f),\tau}
is therefore defined by a single vector
{\bf a}
of size
n_{0}
as in Definition
D.8
of circulant matrices. This vector satisfies
a_{s}=g(2\pi s/n_{0})
for
s=0,\ldots,\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor
, where we recall that
g(d(x,y))=f(x,y)
in the geometric setting. From the Lipschitz condition (
6
), we deduce that
{\bf a}
satisfies some kind of weak non-increasing condition, that is
a_{t}\geq a_{s}\geq 0
for all
0\leq t<s\leq\lfloor n_{0}/2\rfloor
such that
s-t\geq C_{le}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
.

From the bi-Lipschitz condition (
5
), it is easy to see that
{\bf a}
can be uniformly approximated by a non-increasing vector
{\bf a}^{\prime}
such that
\max_{j}|a_{j}-a^{\prime}_{j}|\leq C_{lLe}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
.
Denoting
R
the circulant circular R-matrix based on the vector
{\bf a}^{\prime}
, this means that
\max_{ij}|R_{ij}-f(x_{\tau(i)},x_{\tau(j)})|\leq C_{lLe}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\kern 5.0pt.
Hence,

\|F_{({\bf x}_{S},f)}-R_{\tau^{-1}}\|_{\infty}=\|F_{({\bf x}_{S},f),\tau}-R\|_{\infty}\leq C_{lLe}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

The first result of Lemma
D.11
is a consequence of (
75
) and the last display, setting
\sigma=\tau^{-1}.

Next, by definition of
\tau
, the vector
({\bf x}_{S})_{\sigma^{-1}}=({\bf x}_{S})_{\tau}
is ordered, and it therefore equals any other ordered vector in
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n_{0}}
up to an orthogonal transformation. Hence, we have
[({\bf x}_{S})_{\sigma^{-1}}]_{i}=Q({u}^{(1)}_{i},{v}^{(1)}_{i})
for some orthogonal transformation
Q
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by definition of
{\bf u}^{(1)}
and
{\bf v}^{(1)}
. Equivalently, we have
({\bf x}_{S})_{i}=Q(({\bf u}_{\sigma}^{(1)})_{i},({\bf v}_{\sigma}^{(1)})_{i})
. Then, we conclude again from (
74
) that the vector
{\bf x}^{**}_{S}:=({\bf u}_{\sigma}^{(1)},{\bf v}_{\sigma}^{(1)})
satisfies the second result of the lemma.

∎

Proof of Lemma
D.12
.

Since
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}e^{\iota 4\pi k/n_{0}}=0
, we have
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\cos(4\pi k/n_{0})=0
and
\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\sin(4\pi k/n_{0})

=0
. Then, combining with the trigonometric formulas,
\cos(2x)=2\cos^{2}(x)-1
, and
\sin(2x)=2\cos(x)\sin(x)
, we get

\displaystyle\|{\bf u}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\cos^{2}(2\pi k/n_{0})=\frac{n_{0}}{2}\kern 5.0pt,

(76)

\displaystyle\langle{\bf u}^{(1)},{\bf v}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}\cos(2\pi k/n_{0})\sin(2\pi k/n_{0})=0\kern 5.0pt.

Besides,
\|{\bf u}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}+\|{\bf v}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}=n_{0}
since
(u^{(1)}_{i},v^{(1)}_{i})
for any
i\in[n_{0}]
is a point of the unit sphere
\mathcal{C}
. The combination with (
76
) leads to
\|{\bf v}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}=n_{0}/2
.
∎

D.5
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
D.11
, we consider a vector
{\bf x}\in\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
achieving
d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},{\bf x})=d_{\infty}({\bf x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n})\leq C_{a}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. By the Bi-Lipschitz condition, the matrices
F_{{\bf x^{*}},f}
and
F_{{\bf x},f}
satisfy

\|F_{{\bf x},f}-F_{{\bf x^{*}},f}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{Lea}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
f
is geometric and
{\bf x}
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{n}
, it follows that, up to a permutation,
F_{{\bf x},f}
is a symmetric circulant matrix associated to the vector
a_{j}=g(j\frac{2\pi}{n})
for
j=0,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
. It then follows from Lemma
D.9
, that the eigenvalues of
F_{{\bf x},f}
are equal to the discrete Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{m,n}(g)
of
g
.

The sequence
(a_{j})
, for
j=0,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
is not non-increasing because the function
g
is not exactly decreasing with respect to the distance. Still, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
D.11
, we can build an non-increasing sequence
{\bf a}^{\prime}
satisfying
\max_{j}|a_{j}-a^{\prime}_{j}|\leq C_{lLe}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. The eigenvalues of the corresponding circulant and circular
R
-matrix
R
are also given by Lemma
D.9
. We denote them
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
. It follows from the definition of the Fourier transform that

|\mathcal{F}_{m,n}(g)-\alpha_{m}|\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad 0\leq m\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, the gaps in the Fourier transform
\Phi_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{0,n}(g)-\mathcal{F}_{1,n}(g)
and

\Phi_{2}=\min_{j=2,\ldots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\ \,\mathcal{F}_{1,n}(g)-\mathcal{F}_{j,n}(g)
satisfy

\Big|\Phi_{1}-(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1})\Big|\vee\Big|\Phi_{2}-\underset{m=2,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}{\textup{min}}\,(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{m})\Big|\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

To conclude, it remains to prove that

\displaystyle\Big|(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1})-\Delta_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\ ;

(77)

\displaystyle\Big|\underset{m=2,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}{\textup{min}}\,(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{m})-\Delta_{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

By Lemma
D.10
, we have
\alpha_{0}>\alpha_{1}\geq\max_{j=2,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\alpha_{j}
. Hence, if we denote
\lambda_{0}\geq\lambda_{1}\ldots\geq\lambda_{n-1}
the ordered eigenvalues of
R
we have

\lambda_{0}=\alpha_{0}\geq\lambda_{1}=\alpha_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}=\alpha_{1}\geq\lambda_{3}\kern 5.0pt,

(78)

where
\lambda_{3}=\max\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\}
. By definition of
{\bf a}^{\prime}
and
{\bf x}
, there exists a permutation
\tau
of
[n]
such that

\|F_{{\bf x}^{*},f}-R_{\tau}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

which implies that
\|F_{{\bf x}^{*},f}-R_{\tau}\|_{op}\leq\|F_{{\bf x}^{*},f}-R_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}
. Denoting
\lambda_{0}^{*}\geq\lambda_{1}^{*}\geq\lambda_{2}^{*}\geq\ldots
the ordered eigenvalues of
F_{{\bf x}^{*},f}
, we deduce from Weyl’s inequality that

\max_{j=0,\ldots,n-1}|\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|\leq C_{lLea}\sqrt{n\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt.

Together with (
78
), we deduce that
\Delta_{1}=\lambda_{0}^{*}-\lambda_{1}^{*}
and
\Delta_{2}=\lambda_{2}^{*}-\lambda_{3}^{*}
satisfy (
77
) which concludes the proof.

D.6
Proof of Corollary
4.3
(spectral gap for affine functions)

We will show that, for
n
large enough, the gaps in the Fourier
\Phi_{1}:=\mathcal{F}_{0,n}(g)-\mathcal{F}_{1,n}(g)
and

\Phi_{2}:=\min_{j=2,\ldots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\ \,\mathcal{F}_{1,n}(g)-\mathcal{F}_{j,n}(g)
are at least of the the order of
n
. Corollary
4.3
will then follow directly from Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
.

Recall that the
m
-th coefficient Fourier transform is defined as

\mathcal{F}_{m,n}(g)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}g\left(j\frac{2\pi}{n}\right)\cos\left(j\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

For simplicity, we only consider the case where
n
is odd – the case of even
n
being similar. Let
n=2p+1
with
p\geq 2
. Using the fact that
g(x)=1-x/(2\pi)
, we get

\mathcal{F}_{m,n}(g)=1+2\sum_{j=1}^{p}(1-\frac{j}{n})\cos\left(j\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\qquad m=0,\ldots,p\kern 5.0pt.

(79)

For convenience,
\mathcal{F}_{m,n}(g)
is denoted by
\alpha_{m}
in the sequel.
Let us show that, for
n
large enough,
(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1})\gtrsim n
and
\min_{m\geq 2}(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{m})\gtrsim n
. For
m=0
,

\alpha_{0}=1+2p-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}j=1+2p-\frac{p(p+1)}{n}=n-\frac{(n-1)(n+1)}{4n}=\frac{3n}{4}+\frac{1}{4n}\kern 5.0pt.

(80)

For
m\geq 1
, we can still work out explicitly
\alpha_{m}
.

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}=-2\sum_{j=0}^{p}\frac{j}{n}\cos\left(j\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n}Re\left[\sum_{j=1}^{p}je^{\iota j2\pi m/n}\right]

(81)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n}Re\left[-\iota f^{\prime}\left(\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where the function
f
is defined as
f(x):=\sum_{j=0}^{p}e^{\iota jx}=\frac{e^{\iota(p+1)x}-1}{e^{\iota x}-1}
for
x\in(0,\pi)
. We work out
f^{\prime}(x)
:

\displaystyle-\iota f^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(p+1)e^{\iota(p+1)x}(e^{\iota x}-1)-e^{\iota x}(e^{\iota(p+1)x}-1)}{(e^{\iota x}-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(p+1)e^{\iota px}(e^{\iota x}-1)-(e^{\iota(p+1)x}-1)}{4\sin^{2}(x/2)},

\displaystyle=-\iota\frac{(p+1)e^{\iota(p+\frac{1}{2})x}\sin(\frac{x}{2})-e^{\iota\frac{p+1}{2}x}\sin(\frac{p+1}{2}x)}{2\sin^{2}(x/2)},

where the second line follows from
(e^{\iota x}-1)^{2}=-4\sin^{2}(x/2)e^{\iota x}
. Hence,

\displaystyle Re\left[-\iota f^{\prime}(x)\right]=\frac{(p+1)\sin((p+\frac{1}{2})x)\sin(\frac{x}{2})-\sin^{2}(\frac{p+1}{2}x)}{2\sin^{2}(x/2)}\kern 5.0pt.

Taking
x_{m}=\frac{2\pi m}{n}
, the first term of the numerator is equal to zero since
\sin((p+\frac{1}{2})x_{m})=\sin(\pi m)=0
. Then, combining the above with (
81
) yields

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}=-\frac{2}{n}Re\left[-\iota f^{\prime}\left(\frac{2\pi m}{n}\right)\right]=\frac{\sin^{2}(\frac{p+1}{2}x_{m})}{n\sin^{2}(\tfrac{\pi m}{n})}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\frac{p+1}{2}x_{m}=m\frac{\pi}{2}+m\frac{\pi}{2n}
,

\alpha_{m}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\cos^{2}(\frac{m\pi}{2n})}{n\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi m}{n})}&\mbox{if}\,m\textup{ is odd,}\\
&\mbox{}\\
\frac{\sin^{2}(\frac{m\pi}{2n})}{n\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi m}{n})}&\mbox{if}\,m\textup{ is even.}\end{array}\right.

Hence, the sequence of eigenvalues with odd indices is decreasing:

\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{3}>\alpha_{5}>\ldots>\ldots\kern 5.0pt,

since the fraction
\cos^{2}(m\pi/2n)/\sin^{2}(\pi m/n)
decreases with
m\in[p]
. In the (remaining) case of even indices, the numerator can be upper bounded as follows:
\sin^{2}(\frac{m\pi}{2n})\leq\sin^{2}(\pi/4)\leq\textup{min}_{k\in[p]}\cos^{2}(\frac{k\pi}{2n})
, which leads to
\alpha_{2r}\leq\alpha_{2r-1}
for all
r=1,\ldots,\lfloor p/2\rfloor
.

In other words, each eigenvalue of even index is upper bounded by the previous eigenvalue. In light of this, we only need to prove that, for
n
large enough,

(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1})\wedge(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\wedge(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3})\gtrsim n\kern 5.0pt.

From (
80
), we deduce that
\alpha_{0}
is equivalent to
3n/4
. Besides, we deduce from the explicit form of
\alpha_{m}
in the general case that
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
, and
\alpha_{3}
are respectively equivalent to
\frac{n}{\pi^{2}}
,
1/(4n)
, and
n/(4\pi^{2})
. This completes the proof.

\square

D.7
Proof of Proposition
4.4

For the first inequality of the proposition, the proof is the same as for Proposition
D.1
, after replacing
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}}_{S},{\bf x}^{*}_{S}
,
A_{SS}
respectively by
\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\textrm{VSA}},{\bf x}^{*}
,
A
. The second inequality of the proposition follows from Lemma
4.2
.
\square

E
Probabilistic inequalities

We recall Hoeffding inequality for hypergeometric distributions.

Lemma E.1
.

For
N\geq 1
,
p\in[0,1]
and
n\geq N
, let
X
be a hypergeometric random variable with parameters
(N,p,n)
. Then, for all
t>0
,

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left(|X-Np|\geq\sqrt{\frac{Nt}{2}}\right)\leq 2e^{-t}\kern 5.0pt.

(82)
